) admits a bound on the transition error.

Algorithm 6

Refit

1:

Layer
h
, policy emulator
\widehat{M}
, confidence sets
\mathcal{P}
, test policies
\{\Pi^{\mathsf{test}}_{h}\}_{h\in[H]}
, parameters
\varepsilon>0
and
\delta\in(0,1)
.

2:

Set
\epsilon_{\mathsf{tol}}\coloneqq 80\cdot H\varepsilon
,
n_{\mathsf{mc}}\asymp\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot\log\tfrac{C_{\mathsf{push}}SAH\lvert\Pi\rvert}{\varepsilon\delta}

3:

for
every
(x,x^{\prime})\in\mathcal{X}_{h}[\widehat{M}]\times\mathcal{X}_{h}[\widehat{M}]

do
:
//
Construct candidate test policies at layer
h

4:

Define
\pi_{x,x^{\prime}}\leftarrow\argmax_{\pi\in\mathcal{A}\circ\Pi_{h+1:H}}\lvert\widehat{V}^{\pi}(x)-\widehat{V}^{\pi}(x^{\prime})\rvert
.

5:

Estimate:
//
Verify accuracy of test policies

\displaystyle V_{\mathsf{mc}}(x\mid\pi_{x,x^{\prime}})\leftarrow\mathsf{MC}(x,\pi_{x,x^{\prime}},n_{\mathsf{mc}}),\quad V_{\mathsf{mc}}(x^{\prime}\mid\pi_{x,x^{\prime}})\leftarrow\mathsf{MC}(x^{\prime},\pi_{x,x^{\prime}},n_{\mathsf{mc}})

6:

Set
\mathsf{Violations}\leftarrow\{(x,\pi)\text{\penalty\ estimated in \hyperref@@ii[line:mc1]{line\penalty\ \ref*{line:mc1}} such that\penalty\ }\lvert V_{\mathsf{mc}}(x\mid\pi)-\widehat{V}^{\pi}(x)\rvert\geq\epsilon_{\mathsf{tol}}\}
.

7:

if

\mathsf{Violations}=\varnothing

then

//
No violations found, so return test policies.

8:

Set
\Pi^{\mathsf{test}}_{h}=\cup_{x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}_{h}[\widehat{M}]}\{\pi_{x,x^{\prime}}\}
and
Return

(h-1,\widehat{M},\mathcal{P},\{\Pi^{\mathsf{test}}_{h}\}_{h\in[H]})
.

9:

else

//
Refit transitions to handle violations

10:

for
every
(x,\pi)\in\mathsf{Violations}

do

11:

for
each
(\bar{x},\bar{a})\in\mathcal{X}_{h:H}[\widehat{M}]\times\mathcal{A}
: Estimate
Q_{\mathsf{mc}}(\bar{x},\bar{a}\mid\pi)\leftarrow\mathsf{MC}(\bar{x},\bar{a}\circ\pi,n_{\mathsf{mc}})
.

12:

Define for every
(\bar{x},\bar{a})\in\mathcal{X}_{h:H}[\widehat{M}]\times\mathcal{A}
:

\displaystyle\Delta(\bar{x},\bar{a})\coloneqq\widehat{R}(\bar{x},\bar{a})+\En_{x^{\prime}\sim\widehat{P}(\cdot\mid\bar{x},\bar{a})}\left[Q_{\mathsf{mc}}(x^{\prime},\pi(x^{\prime})\mid\pi)\right]-Q_{\mathsf{mc}}(\bar{x},\bar{a}\mid\pi).

13:

for
every
(\bar{x},\bar{a})
such that
\lvert\Delta(\bar{x},\bar{a})\rvert\geq\epsilon_{\mathsf{tol}}/(8H)

do
:

//
Define loss vectors, overwriting if already defined.

14:

Set
\ell_{\mathrm{loss}}(\bar{x},\bar{a})\coloneqq\mathrm{sign}(\Delta(\bar{x},\bar{a}))\cdot Q_{\mathsf{mc}}(\cdot,\pi(\cdot)\mid\pi)\in[0,1]^{\mathcal{X}_{h(\bar{x})+1}[\widehat{M}]}

//
OMD update with negative entropy Bregman Divergence on violations.

15:

for
every
(\bar{x},\bar{a})
from
line
14
: Update

\displaystyle\widehat{P}(\cdot\mid\bar{x},\bar{a})\leftarrow\argmin_{p\in\mathcal{P}(\bar{x},\bar{a})}\penalty\ \left\langle p,\ell_{\mathrm{loss}}\left(\bar{x},\bar{a}\right)\right\rangle+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\cdot D_{\mathsf{ne}}\left(p\penalty\ \|\penalty\ \widehat{P}(\cdot\mid\bar{x},\bar{a})\right)

16:

Return

(\ell,\widehat{M},\mathcal{P},\{\Pi^{\mathsf{test}}_{h}\}_{h\in[H]})
where
\ell
is the maximum layer s.t.
(\bar{x},\bar{a})\in\mathcal{X}_{\ell}\times\mathcal{A}
was updated in
line
15
.

6.3
Refit
Subroutine

Now we discuss
Refit
. The skeleton is the same as in
Refit.D
: once the transition functions for
\widehat{M}
have been estimated for a given layer
h
,
Refit
attempts to compute a set of valid test policies
\Pi^{\mathsf{test}}_{h}
for pairs of observations (see
Definition
14
). If it cannot, this implies that at least one transition that we previously estimated in layer
h
onward must have been incorrectly estimated, and we search for it starting in
line
11
. In this case, we revisit the maximum layer where some transition was updated and restart the decoding procedure.

OMD Regret as a Potential Function.

Our main innovation to control the number of refitting iterations is to design the right potential function. In
PLHR.D
, since we were working with deterministic transitions, we used the size of
\mathcal{P}(s,a)
as the potential function. Since we are now estimating
\widehat{P}(\cdot\mid x,a)
in a continuous space, this idea does not extend.

Instead, we use the regret of online mirror descent (OMD) against the competitor vector
\mathsf{Proj}(P_{\mathsf{lat}}(\cdot\mid x,a))
as the potential function. We show that every transition in
line
15
witnesses constant regret with respect to
\mathsf{Proj}(P(\cdot\mid x,a))
. In our analysis, we maintain the invariant property that
\mathcal{P}
is just big enough so that
\mathsf{Proj}(P(\cdot\mid x,a))\in\mathcal{P}
throughout the execution of
PLHR
. Therefore, the standard analysis of OMD
[
11
, see, e.g.,]
gives us an upper bound on the cumulative regret. Letting
T_{\mathrm{refit}}
denote the number of updates on a given
(x,a)
pair, we have

\displaystyle\varepsilon\cdot T_{\mathrm{refit}}\lesssim\text{Regret of OMD}\lesssim\sqrt{\log\lvert\mathcal{X}[\widehat{M}]\rvert\cdot T_{\mathrm{refit}}}.

Rearranging, we get a bound on the number of updates
T_{\mathrm{refit}}
for any
(x,a)
, and since the total number of states in the policy emulator
\widehat{M}
is bounded, we get a bound on the total number of updates made by
Refit
.

7
Discussion

Our results show interesting trade-offs between representational conditions and environment access for achieving sample-efficient policy learning. When the environment access is either the generative model or
\mu
-resets, we show lower bounds which illustrate the challenge of agnostic policy learning in MDPs with large state spaces. On the positive side, we give a new algorithm PLHR which leverages hybrid resets to efficiently learn Block MDPs; this is accomplished via a new technical tool called the policy emulator. We highlight several open problems:

•

Extending the Positive Result:
Can
Theorem
4
be extended to more general settings? While we establish that policy emulators of bounded size exist for pushforward coverable MDPs (
Appendix
C
), we do not know how to efficiently construct them. One natural class of problems to study is the low-rank MDP, which generalizes the Block MDP and also satisfies low (pushforward) coverability. An algorithm achieving
\mathrm{poly}(d)
sample complexity would showcase the power of hybrid resets, as prior work
[
46
]
shows that
\exp(d)
sample complexity is necessary and sufficient for agnostic RL in low-rank MDPs with just online access.
Another direction for improving
Theorem
4
is replacing the dependence on pushforward concentrability with the smaller concentrability. Unfortunately, our guarantee for
PLHR
breaks down because it uses pushforward concentrability to enable accurate policy emulation of the transitions from every state in the emulator.

•

Benefits of Realizability:
Is it possible to achieve positive results for the
\mu
-reset model with policy realizability (thus directly improving upon
PSDP
and contrasting with our lower bound
Theorem
3
)? This question can be viewed as the policy-based analogue of the question raised by
[
38
]
on whether it is possible to achieve sample-efficient learning with standard online access if one assumes only coverability and value function realizability (
Q^{\star}\in\mathcal{F}
).

Appendix A
Additional Related Works

Access Models in RL.

The
\mu
-reset access setting was introduced in
[
32
,
29
]
, and is widely studied in the policy learning literature
[
7
,
8
,
1
]
. We refer the reader to Appendix A of
[
38
]
for an exemplary survey of related works on local/global simulators, both theoretical and empirical. As a summary, in terms of theory, the study of local simulator access has mostly focused on linear function approximation settings, where it is shown that state revisiting enables one to circumvent statistical lower bounds for online RL, or enables computationally efficient approaches which are not known to exist for online RL. Generative model (or global simulator) access has mostly been studied for tabular or linear settings.

Algorithms for Policy Learning.

We highlight several algorithms for policy learning in large state spaces. For abstract policy classes, the predominant approaches are Policy Search by Dynamic Programming (
PSDP
)
[
9
]
and Conservative Policy Iteration (
CPI
)
[
32
]
(see also
[
44
,
47
]
). In particular,
PSDP
is a backbone of many contemporary theoretical works in RL
[
39
,
54
,
4
,
37
,
36
, see e.g.,]
. Both
PSDP
and
CPI
operate under the
\mu
-reset setting, assume policy completeness, and achieve similar guarantees (see discussion in
Appendix
B
). The agnostic policy learning setting (where representational conditions such as policy completeness are
not
assumed) was initiated by
[
33
,
29
]
and has recently received more attention in the papers
[
46
,
24
]
.

Specializing to smoothly-parameterized policy classes
\Pi=\{\pi_{\theta}\}_{\theta\in\Theta}
, many works have studied policy gradient methods such as REINFORCE
[
56
]
, Policy Gradient
[
49
]
, and Natural Policy Gradient
[
28
]
. Empirically this has given rise to state-of-the-art algorithms for policy optimization
[
48
,
52
]
. In terms of theory, a line of work studies policy gradient methods
[
5
,
61
,
35
,
45
]
for the restricted setting of linear MDPs
[
27
]
, designing algorithms which do not require
\mu
-reset access (note that policy completeness is naturally satisfied for linear MDPs). Going beyond linear MDPs, the papers
[
10
,
21
]
study policy gradient methods but require
\mu
-reset access as well some type of completeness/closure assumptions for global
optimality guarantees.

Coverage Conditions.

Coverage conditions have been extensively studied in RL. In offline RL, many works study the concentrability coefficient
[
41
,
40
,
13
,
19
,
25
]
as well as weaker notions such as single-policy concentrability
[
26
,
43
]
, conditions based on value-function approximation
[
13
,
58
]
, and approximate notions for continuous dynamics
[
53
]
. In addition, under the
\mu
-reset model, the standard assumption made is on bounded concentrability coefficient, sometimes called the
distribution mismatch coefficient

[
7
]
. More recently,
[
59
]
introduced the notion of
coverability coefficient
and study it for standard online RL access with value function approximation. Coverability (and the related pushforward variant) is further studied in the papers
[
4
,
3
,
2
,
24
,
38
]
.

Block MDPs.

Block MDPs are a canonical model for studying reinforcement learning with large state spaces but low intrinsic complexity. In particular, Block MDPs are known to satisfy low (pushforward) coverability
[
38
]
, implying that reset distributions exist which satisfy low (pushforward) concentrability. They have been studied in a long line of work
[
22
,
15
,
39
,
62
,
55
,
37
]
. Recently,
[
2
]
study a more general setting of RL with latent dynamics which covers the Block MDP as a special case. A common theme among these works is that standard online access to
M
is assumed, and the assumption of
decoder realizability
is made, i.e., that the learner is given access to a class
\Phi
such that
\phi\in\Phi
, with the achievable bounds scaling with
\log\lvert\Phi\rvert
. Under standard online access, a minimax lower bound of
\log\lvert\Phi\rvert
can be obtained by reduction to supervised learning. In contrast, our work studies how to achieve sample-efficient learning without decoder realizability but with
stronger forms of access
to
M
. Our bounds replace the dependence on
\log\lvert\Phi\rvert
(which in the worst case can scale with
\lvert\mathcal{X}\rvert
) with dependence on
\log\lvert\Pi\rvert
, which can be arbitrarily smaller.

Appendix B
Background and Additional Results for
PSDP

In this section, we provide a description of the
PSDP
algorithm and analyze its sample complexity. We show the standard upper bound for
PSDP
which has appeared in prior works
[
39
, e.g.,]
in
Appendix
B.1
. We also prove several new results about
PSDP
when only policy realizability is satisfied: namely if the reset distribution
\mu
satisfies stronger properties beyond bounded concentrability, we show exponential in
H
upper bounds in
Appendix
B.2
as well as a matching lower bound in
Appendix
B.3
. We also discuss in
Appendix
B.3
how our lower bounds against
PSDP
also apply against the
CPI
algorithm, as claimed in the main text.

B.1
PSDP
Guarantee Under Policy Completeness

First, we define an averaged notion of policy completeness; compared to
Definition
2
, this notion is weaker since it only requires completeness to hold in an averaged sense over the reset
\mu
.

For readability, we slightly abuse notation: for
Q
-functions we denote
Q^{\pi}_{h}(x,\pi)\coloneqq Q^{\pi}_{h}(x,\pi(x))
. Similarly, we sometimes denote rewards as
R(x,\pi)\coloneqq R(x,\pi(x))
and transitions as
P(\cdot\mid x,\pi)\coloneqq P(\cdot\mid x,\pi(x))
.

Definition 9

(Average Policy Completeness)
.

Fix any policy class
\Pi
, as well as exploratory distribution
\mu=\{\mu_{h}\}_{h\in[H]}
. For any layer
h\in[H]
and policy
{\widehat{\pi}}\coloneqq{\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H}\in\Pi_{h+1:H}
we define the (average)
policy completeness error
, denoted
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}:\Pi_{h+1:H}\to\mathbb{R}
, as

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}})\coloneqq\min_{\pi_{h}\in\Pi_{h}}\penalty\ \En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\penalty\ Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,a)-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\pi_{h})\right].

Definition
9
is similar to previously defined notions of policy completeness
[
47
,
1
]
. As a point of comparison, Definition 2 of
[
1
]
defines the average policy completeness to be the worst case over the convex hull of suffix policies
{\widehat{\pi}}
, i.e.
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}\coloneqq\sup_{{\widehat{\pi}}\in\mathsf{Conv}(\Pi)}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}})
, while we define it as a function which takes as input a rollout policy
{\widehat{\pi}}
.

We state the
PSDP
algorithm in
Algorithm
7
and then prove
Theorem
1
.

Algorithm 7

PSDP

[
9
]

1:

Reset distributions
\mu=\{\mu_{h}\}_{h\in[H]}
, policy class
\Pi
.

2:

for

h=H,\cdots,1

do

3:

Initialize dataset
\mathcal{D}_{h}=\varnothing
.

4:

for

n
times
do
:
//
Collecting
(x_{h},a_{h},v_{h})
requires
\mu
-reset access.

5:

Sample
(x_{h},a_{h})
where
x_{h}\sim\mu_{h}
and
a_{h}\sim\mathrm{Unif}(\mathcal{A})
.

6:

Let
v_{h}\coloneqq\sum_{h^{\prime}=h}^{H}r_{h^{\prime}}
be the value of executing
a_{h}\circ{\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H}
from
x_{h}
.

7:

Set
\mathcal{D}_{h}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}_{h}\cup\{(x_{h},a_{h},v_{h})\}
.

8:

Call CB oracle:
{\widehat{\pi}}_{h}\coloneqq\argmax_{\pi\in\Pi}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{(x_{h},a_{h},v_{h})\in\mathcal{D}_{h}}\frac{\mathbbm{1}\left\{a_{h}=\pi(x_{h})\right\}}{A}\cdot v_{h}
.

9:

Return

{\widehat{\pi}}_{1:H}
.

Proof of
Theorem
1
.

First, we state a standard generalization bound on the contextual bandit oracle invoked in
line
8
. With probability at least
1-\delta
, for every
h\in[H]
the returned policy
{\widehat{\pi}}_{h}
satisfies

\displaystyle\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,{\widehat{\pi}}_{h})\right]\geq\max_{\pi_{h}\in\Pi_{h}}\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\pi_{h})\right]-\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}},\quad\text{where}\quad\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}\coloneqq O\left(\sqrt{\frac{A\log(\lvert\Pi\rvert/\delta)}{n}}\right).

(8)

For every
h\in[H]
, let us define:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\pi}_{h}^{\star}(x)\coloneqq\argmax_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\penalty\ Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,a),\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{\pi}_{h}\coloneqq\argmax_{\pi_{h}\in\Pi_{h}}\penalty\ \En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\pi_{h})\right]

Then we calculate:

\displaystyle V^{\star}-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{=}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\En_{x\sim d^{\pi^{\star}}_{h}}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\pi^{\star})-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,{\widehat{\pi}}_{h})\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(ii)}{\leq}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\En_{x\sim d^{\pi^{\star}}_{h}}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\widetilde{\pi}_{h}^{\star})-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,{\widehat{\pi}}_{h})\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(iii)}{\leq}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\left\|\frac{d^{\pi^{\star}}_{h}}{\mu_{h}}\right\|_{\infty}\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\widetilde{\pi}_{h}^{\star})-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,{\widehat{\pi}}_{h})\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(iv)}{\leq}C_{\mathsf{conc}}\cdot\sum_{h=1}^{H}\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\widetilde{\pi}_{h}^{\star})-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,{\widehat{\pi}}_{h})\right]

\displaystyle=C_{\mathsf{conc}}\cdot\sum_{h=1}^{H}\left(\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\widetilde{\pi}_{h}^{\star})-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\widetilde{\pi}_{h})\right]+\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\widetilde{\pi}_{h})-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,{\widehat{\pi}}_{h})\right]\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(v)}{\leq}HC_{\mathsf{conc}}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}+C_{\mathsf{conc}}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H}).

Here,
(i)
follows by the Performance Difference Lemma,
(ii)
is due to the optimality of
\widetilde{\pi}^{\star}_{h}
,
(iii)
is due to nonnegativity of
Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\widetilde{\pi}_{h}^{\star})-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,{\widehat{\pi}}_{h})
,
(iv)
is due to the definition of
C_{\mathsf{conc}}
, and
(v)
follows by
Definition
9
and Eq. (
8
). Therefore, if the policy completeness error is zero, then we have a bound which is at most
HC_{\mathsf{conc}}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}
, and therefore
PSDP
returns an
\varepsilon
-optimal policy using
\mathrm{poly}(C_{\mathsf{conc}},A,H,\log\lvert\Pi\rvert,\varepsilon^{-1},\log\delta^{-1})
samples.
∎

B.2
Upper Bounds for
PSDP
with Policy Realizability

As shown by the example in
Figure
1
, without policy completeness,
PSDP
may not even be consistent, since one can take
\gamma
to be arbitrarily close to 0 so that with constant probability
PSDP
returns a
(1+\gamma)
-suboptimal policy. In this section, we circumvent the lower bound and show that if we make stronger assumptions on the reset distribution
\mu
,
PSDP
achieves consistency:

1.

If
\Pi
is realizable and the reset
\mu
has bounded pushforward concentrability,
Theorem
6
achieves
(C_{\mathsf{push}})^{O(H)}
sample complexity.

2.

If
\Pi
is realizable and the reset
\mu
is admissible (
Definition
10
) and has bounded concentrability,
Theorem
7
achieves
(C_{\mathsf{conc}})^{O(H)}
sample complexity.

The two upper bounds are in general incomparable, as there exist settings in which one achieves a better guarantee than the other. In addition, to the best of our knowledge, neither result is implied by any known results for policy learning—note that the trivial bound of
A^{H}
achieved by importance sampling
[
33
,
6
]
can be much larger when
C_{\mathsf{push}}\ll A
.

B.2.1
Policy Realizability + Pushforward Concentrability

Theorem 6
.

Suppose
\Pi
is realizable, and the reset
\mu
satisfies pushforward concentrability with parameter
C_{\mathsf{push}}
. With high probability,
PSDP
returns an
\varepsilon
-optimal policy using

\displaystyle\mathrm{poly}((C_{\mathsf{push}})^{H},A,\log\lvert\Pi\rvert,\varepsilon^{-1})\quad\text{samples.}

The proof relies on the following lemma, which relates the policy completeness error to the pushforward concentrability coefficient of
\mu
.

Lemma 1
.

Fix any layer
h\in[H]
. For any suffix policy
{\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H}
we have

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H})\leq C_{\mathsf{push}}\cdot\En_{x^{\prime}\sim\mu_{h+1}}\left[V^{\star}(x^{\prime})-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x^{\prime})\right].

Proof of
Lemma
1
.

We have the following computation:

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H})

\displaystyle=\min_{\pi_{h}\in\Pi_{h}}\penalty\ \En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\penalty\ Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,a)-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\pi_{h})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\penalty\ Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,a)-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\pi^{\star})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[Q^{\star}(x,\pi^{\star})-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\pi^{\star})\right]

\displaystyle=\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[r(x,\pi^{\star})+\En_{x^{\prime}\sim P(\cdot\mid x,\pi^{\star})}V^{\star}(x^{\prime})\right]-\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[r(x,\pi^{\star})+\En_{x^{\prime}\sim P(\cdot\mid x,\pi^{\star})}V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x^{\prime})\right]

\displaystyle=\En_{x\sim\mu_{h},x^{\prime}\sim P(\cdot\mid x,\pi^{\star})}\left[V^{\star}(x^{\prime})-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x^{\prime})\right].

(9)

The first inequality is due to the realizability
\pi^{\star}\in\Pi
, and the second one is due to the optimality of
\pi^{\star}
.
Now we will perform a change of measure to relate the bound in Eq. (
9
) to the error of
{\widehat{\pi}}
on the layer
h+1
.

\displaystyle\En_{x\sim\mu_{h},x^{\prime}\sim P(\cdot\mid x,\pi^{\star})}\left[V^{\star}(x^{\prime})-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x^{\prime})\right]

\displaystyle=\En_{x^{\prime}\sim\mu_{h+1}}\left[\frac{\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}P(x^{\prime}\mid x,\pi^{\star})}{\mu_{h+1}(x^{\prime})}\cdot\left(V^{\star}(x^{\prime})-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x^{\prime})\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mathsf{push}}\cdot\En_{x^{\prime}\sim\mu_{h+1}}\left[V^{\star}(x^{\prime})-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x^{\prime})\right],

where the inequality uses the nonnegativity of
V^{\star}(x^{\prime})-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x^{\prime})
and the definition of pushforward concentrability. Plugging this back into Eq. (
9
) proves
Lemma
1
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem
6
.

Using Performance Difference Lemma we have for the learned policy
{\widehat{\pi}}\in\Pi
:

\displaystyle V^{\star}-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{h=1}^{H}\En_{x\sim d^{{\widehat{\pi}}}_{h}}\left[V^{\star}(x)-Q^{\star}(x,{\widehat{\pi}}_{h})\right]=\sum_{h=1}^{H}\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[\frac{d^{{\widehat{\pi}}_{h}}(x)}{\mu_{h}(x)}\left(V^{\star}(x)-Q^{\star}(x,{\widehat{\pi}}_{h})\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mathsf{conc}}\cdot\sum_{h=1}^{H}\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[V^{\star}(x)-Q^{\star}(x,{\widehat{\pi}})\right]\leq C_{\mathsf{conc}}\cdot\sum_{h=1}^{H}\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[V^{\star}(x)-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x)\right].

The first inequality uses the fact that
{\widehat{\pi}}\in\Pi
as well as
V^{\star}(x)\geq Q^{\star}(x,{\widehat{\pi}}_{h})
, and the second inequality uses the latter fact again. From here, we apply an inductive argument to bound the suboptimality
\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}[V^{\star}(x)-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x)]
for all
h\in[H]
. Fix any
h\in[H]
. We have

\displaystyle\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[V^{\star}(x)-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x)\right]

\displaystyle=\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[Q^{\star}(x,\pi^{\star})-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,{\widehat{\pi}})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[Q^{\star}(x,\pi^{\star})-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\pi^{\star})+\max_{a}Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,a)-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,{\widehat{\pi}})\right]

\displaystyle=\En_{x\sim\mu_{h},x^{\prime}\sim P(\cdot\mid x,\pi^{\star})}\left[V^{\star}(x^{\prime})-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x^{\prime})\right]+\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[\max_{a}Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,a)-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,{\widehat{\pi}})\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mathsf{push}}\En_{x^{\prime}\sim\mu_{h+1}}\left[V^{\star}(x^{\prime})-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x^{\prime})\right]+\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}+\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H})

\displaystyle\leq 2C_{\mathrm{push}}\cdot\En_{x^{\prime}\sim\mu_{h+1}}\left[V^{\star}(x^{\prime})-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x^{\prime})\right]+\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}},

(10)

where the last inequality uses
Lemma
1
. Recursive application of
(
10
)
and the fact that
\En_{x\sim\mu_{H}}[V^{\star}(x)-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x)]=\En_{x\sim\mu_{H}}[r(x,\pi^{\star})-r(x,{\widehat{\pi}}_{H})]\leq\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}
gives us

\displaystyle\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[V^{\star}(x)-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x)\right]\leq H\cdot(2C_{\mathsf{push}})^{H}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}},

so therefore the final suboptimality of
PSDP
is at most

\displaystyle V^{\star}-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}\leq C_{\mathsf{conc}}\cdot\sum_{h=1}^{H}\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[V^{\star}(x)-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x)\right]\leq H^{2}\cdot(2C_{\mathsf{push}})^{H+1}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}.

Choosing
n=\mathrm{poly}((C_{\mathsf{push}})^{H},A,\log\lvert\Pi\rvert,\varepsilon^{-1})
so that the right hand side is at most
\varepsilon
proves the final bound.
∎

B.2.2
Policy Realizability + Admissibility + Concentrability

Definition 10
.

We say a distribution
\mu
is admissible if for every
h\in[H]
there exists some
\pi_{b}\in\Delta(\Pi)
:

\displaystyle\mu_{h}(x)=d^{\pi_{b}}_{h}(x)\quad\text{for all}\penalty\ x\in\mathcal{X}_{h}.

Theorem 7
.

Suppose
\Pi
is realizable, and the reset
\mu
(1) satisfies concentrability with parameter
C_{\mathsf{conc}}
, and (2) is admissible. With high probability,
PSDP
finds an
\varepsilon
-optimal policy using
\mathrm{poly}((C_{\mathsf{conc}})^{H},A,\log\lvert\Pi\rvert,\varepsilon^{-1})
samples.

To prove
Theorem
7
, we first establish a few helper lemmas on the errors of the learned policy
{\widehat{\pi}}
.

Lemma 2
.

For any layer
h\in[H]
and admissible distribution
\nu\in\Delta(\mathcal{X}_{h})
, we have

\displaystyle\max_{\pi\in\Pi_{h}}\penalty\ \En_{x\sim\nu}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\pi)-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,{\widehat{\pi}})\right]\leq C_{\mathsf{conc}}\left(\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}+\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H})\right).

Proof.

We calculate that

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\max_{\pi\in\Pi_{h}}\penalty\ \En_{x\sim\nu}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\pi)-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,{\widehat{\pi}}_{h})\right]

\displaystyle=\max_{\pi\in\Pi_{h}}\En_{x\sim\nu}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\pi)-\max_{a}Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,a)\right]+\En_{x\sim\nu}\left[\max_{a}Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,a)-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,{\widehat{\pi}}_{h})\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mathsf{conc}}\cdot\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[\max_{a}Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,a)-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,{\widehat{\pi}}_{h})\right]

\displaystyle=C_{\mathsf{conc}}\cdot\Bigg(\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[\max_{a}Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,a)\right]-\max_{\pi\in\Pi_{h}}\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\pi)\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 80.00012pt+\max_{\pi\in\Pi_{h}}\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\pi)\right]-\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,{\widehat{\pi}}_{h})\right]\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mathsf{conc}}\left(\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}+\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H})\right).

In the first inequality we use the fact that
\nu
is admissible, so we can use concentrability to relate the density ratios
\|\nu/\mu\|_{\infty}
.
∎

Additional Notation.
In the subsequent analysis, for any distribution
\nu
we denote
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}},\nu)
to be the policy completeness error under distribution
\nu
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}},\nu)\coloneqq\min_{\pi_{h}\in\Pi_{h}}\penalty\ \En_{x\sim\nu}\left[\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\penalty\ Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,a)-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\pi)\right].

For any partial policy
\pi_{h:t-1}
, we also denote
\nu\circ\pi_{h:t-1}\in\Delta(\mathcal{X}_{t})
to denote the distribution over states in layer
t
which is achieved by first sampling a state
x_{h}\sim\nu
then rolling out with partial policy
\pi_{h:t-1}
.

Lemma 3
.

For any layer
h\in[H]
and admissible distribution
\nu\in\Delta(\mathcal{X}_{h})
, we have

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H},\nu)\leq(H-h)\cdot C_{\mathsf{conc}}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}+C_{\mathsf{conc}}\cdot\sum_{h^{\prime}=h+1}^{H}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h^{\prime}+1:H})

Proof.

Using the definition of policy completeness we have

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H},\nu)

\displaystyle\leq\En_{x\sim\nu}\left[\max_{a}Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,a)-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\pi^{\star})\right]\leq\En_{x\sim\nu}\left[Q^{\star}(x,\pi^{\star})-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\pi^{\star})\right].

Now, we apply a recursive argument, which gives us

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H},\nu)

\displaystyle\leq\En_{x\sim\nu}\left[Q^{\star}(x,\pi^{\star})-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\pi^{\star})\right]

\displaystyle=\En_{x^{\prime}\sim\nu\circ\pi^{\star}}\left[Q^{\star}(x^{\prime},\pi^{\star})-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x^{\prime},{\widehat{\pi}})\right]

\displaystyle=\En_{x^{\prime}\sim\nu\circ\pi^{\star}}\left[Q^{\star}(x^{\prime},\pi^{\star})-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x^{\prime},\pi^{\star})+Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x^{\prime},\pi^{\star})-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x^{\prime},{\widehat{\pi}})\right]

Because
\nu
is admissible, so is
\nu\circ\pi^{\star}
. Therefore, the second term in the sum is bounded using
Lemma
2
:

\displaystyle\En_{x^{\prime}\sim\nu\circ\pi^{\star}}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x^{\prime},\pi^{\star})-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x^{\prime},{\widehat{\pi}})\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mathsf{conc}}\left(\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h+2:H})+\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}\right).

The first term in the sum can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\En_{x^{\prime}\sim\nu\circ\pi^{\star}}\left[Q^{\star}(x^{\prime},\pi^{\star})-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x^{\prime},\pi^{\star})\right]=\En_{{x^{\prime\prime}}\sim\nu\circ\pi^{\star}\circ\pi^{\star}}\left[Q^{\star}(x^{\prime\prime},\pi^{\star})-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x^{\prime\prime},{\widehat{\pi}})\right].

Applying recursion, we get the final bound of

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H},\nu)

\displaystyle\leq(H-h)\cdot C_{\mathsf{conc}}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}+C_{\mathsf{conc}}\cdot\sum_{h^{\prime}=h+1}^{H}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h^{\prime}+1:H}).

This concludes the proof of
Lemma
3
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem
7
.

We compute the suboptimality as

\displaystyle V^{\star}-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{h=1}^{H}\En_{x\sim d^{\star}_{h}}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\pi^{\star})-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,{\widehat{\pi}})\right]

(Performance Difference Lemma)

\displaystyle\leq HC_{\mathsf{conc}}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}+C_{\mathsf{conc}}\left(\sum_{h=1}^{H}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H})\right).

(
Lemma
2
)

Now we apply
Lemma
3
to show that the policy completeness error can be bounded by the downstream policy completeness errors, using the admissibility of
\mu
.

\displaystyle V^{\star}-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}

\displaystyle\leq HC_{\mathsf{conc}}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}+C_{\mathsf{conc}}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H})

\displaystyle=HC_{\mathsf{conc}}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}+C_{\mathsf{conc}}\cdot\left(\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{2:H})+\sum_{h=2}^{H}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H})\right)

\displaystyle\leq HC_{\mathsf{conc}}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}+C_{\mathsf{conc}}\cdot\left((H-1)C_{\mathsf{conc}}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}+(1+C_{\mathsf{conc}})\cdot\sum_{h=2}^{H}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H})\right)

(
Lemma
3
)

\displaystyle\lesssim H(C_{\mathsf{conc}})^{2}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}+(1+C_{\mathsf{conc}})^{2}\cdot\left(\sum_{h=2}^{H}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H})\right)

\displaystyle=H(C_{\mathsf{conc}})^{2}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}+(1+C_{\mathsf{conc}})^{2}\cdot\left(\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{3:H})+\sum_{h=3}^{H}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H})\right)

\displaystyle\leq H(C_{\mathsf{conc}})^{2}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}

\displaystyle\hskip 30.00005pt+(1+C_{\mathsf{conc}})^{2}\cdot\left((H-2)C_{\mathsf{conc}}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}+(1+C_{\mathsf{conc}})\sum_{h=3}^{H}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H})\right)

(
Lemma
3
)

\displaystyle\lesssim H\left(1+C_{\mathsf{conc}}\right)^{3}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}+(1+C_{\mathsf{conc}})^{3}\left(\sum_{h=3}^{H}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H})\right).

Continuing this way (and observing that
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{PC}}({\widehat{\pi}}_{H+1}=\varnothing)=0
) we get a final bound of

\displaystyle V^{\star}-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}\lesssim H(1+C_{\mathsf{conc}})^{H}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}.

Setting
n=\mathrm{poly}((C_{\mathsf{conc}})^{H},A,\log\lvert\Pi\rvert,\varepsilon^{-1})
makes the RHS at most
\varepsilon
, thus proving
Theorem
7
.
∎

B.3
Lower Bounds for
PSDP
and
CPI

Now we will show that exponential error compounding is unavoidable for
PSDP
in the absence of policy completeness.
PSDP
relies on a reduction to a contextual bandit oracle. For the lower bound statement, we will assume that
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}>0
is a fixed constant and
PSDP
is equipped with a
worst case
oracle
\mathsf{CB}_{\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}}
which for every layer
h\in[H]
always returns an
arbitrary policy

{\widehat{\pi}}_{h}
satisfying

\displaystyle\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,{\widehat{\pi}}_{h})\right]\geq\max_{\pi\in\Pi}\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}}(x,\pi)\right]-\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}.

Thus, the lower bound statement has the flavor of a statistical query lower bound
[
31
]
, which also assumes a worst-case response up to accuracy
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}
.

Theorem 8
.

Let
H\geq 2
. Fix any
\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}>0
and parameter
C_{\mathsf{push}}\geq 5H
. There exists a tabular MDP
M
with
S=O(H^{2})
states,
A=O(H)
actions, and horizon
H
, realizable policy class
\Pi
of size
2^{\widetilde{O}(H)}
, and exploratory distribution
\mu
which is admissible and satisfies pushforward concentrability with parameter
C_{\mathsf{push}}
, so that
PSDP
equipped with oracle
\mathsf{CB}_{\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}}
returns a policy
{\widehat{\pi}}
:

\displaystyle V^{\star}-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}\geq(C_{\mathsf{push}})^{\Omega(H)}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}.

Theorem
8
is a converse to the positive results of
Theorem
6
and
7
, showing that
PSDP
can have exponential in
H
sample complexity. The lower bound construction in
Theorem
8
as well as the earlier one from
Figure
1
are given by tabular MDPs, which our main upper bound in this paper (
Theorem
4
) can solve with polynomial number of samples with
\mu
-reset access. (Note that when the state space
\mathcal{X}
itself is bounded in size,
PLHR
does not require local simulator access because it can perform resets directly using rejection sampling from
\mu
.) Thus,
Theorem
8
indicates an
algorithmic limitation
of using dynamic programming to solve policy learning.

We also remark that the constructions in
Figure
1
and
Theorem
8
also apply to
CPI

[
32
]
; we refer the reader to
[
6
, Section 14 of]
for an exposition of the
CPI
algorithm. At a high level, the
CPI
algorithm generates a sequence of policy iterates
\pi^{(1)},\pi^{(2)},\cdots
such that each policy iterate improves upon the previous one and terminates whenever:

\displaystyle\max_{\widetilde{\pi}\in\Pi}\penalty\ \En_{x\sim d^{\pi^{(t)}}_{\mu}}\left[Q^{\pi^{(t)}}(x,\widetilde{\pi})-Q^{\pi^{(t)}}(x,\pi^{(t)})\right]\leq\varepsilon.

where
d^{\pi^{(t)}}_{\mu}
is the occupancy measure obtained by running the current iterate
\pi^{(t)}
starting from the reset
\mu
and
\varepsilon>0
is some predefined threshold which represents the accuracy to which
CPI
solves the policy improvement problem. Thus, if it is not possible to greatly improve (by at least
\varepsilon
) the average
Q
-function by selecting a different policy
\widetilde{\pi}
, then
CPI
will terminate. In our constructions, one can check that if we initialize to the all-zeros policy
\pi^{(1)}\equiv 0
, then
CPI
will terminate immediately even though
\pi^{(1)}
has constant suboptimality.

In the rest of this section, we will prove
Theorem
8
.

B.3.1
Lower Bound Construction

Figure 6
:
Lower bound construction for
Theorem
8
. To avoid clutter, we do not illustrate the highway states as well as any boring states which are only reachable by taking highway actions
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
at layer 0, since their role is only to make sure that the construction satisfies admissibility.

Our lower bound construction is illustrated in
Figure
6
.

For notational convenience, we number the layers starting with
h=0
, so that there are
H+1
layers.

State and Action Spaces.

At
h=0
there is a single state
x_{\circ}
and the action set is
\mathcal{A}_{0}=\{0,1,\mathfrak{a}_{1},\cdots,\mathfrak{a}_{H}\}
. For
h\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{h}=\underbrace{\left\{x_{h,0},x_{h,1}\cdots x_{h,H}\right\}}_{\text{$H+1$ boring states}}\cup\underbrace{\left\{x_{h,\diamond}\right\}\cup\left\{x_{h,\star}\right\}}_{\text{2 special states}}\cup\underbrace{\left\{\bar{x}_{h\to h+1},\bar{x}_{h\to h+2},\cdots\bar{x}_{h\to H}\right\}}_{\text{$H-h$ highway states}},

except for
\mathcal{X}_{H}
which does not have the special state
x_{h,\diamond}
. The action set is
\mathcal{A}_{h}=\{0,1\}
.

Policy Class.

The policy class
\Pi
is taken to be all open-loop policies over each layer’s action space:

\displaystyle\Pi\coloneqq\left\{\pi:\forall x\in\mathcal{X}_{h},\pi_{h}(x)\equiv a_{h},(a_{0},a_{1},\cdots,a_{H})\in\prod_{h=0}^{H}\mathcal{A}_{h}\right\}.

Reset Distribution.

At layer
h=0
we have
d_{0}=\mu_{0}=\delta_{x_{\circ}}
. At layer
h\geq 1
, the distribution
\mu_{h}
puts
1/C_{\mathsf{push}}
mass on each of the non-diamond states
\{x_{h,0},x_{h,1}\cdots x_{h,H}\}\cup\{x_{h,\star}\}\cup\{\bar{x}_{h\to h+1},\bar{x}_{h\to h+2},\cdots\bar{x}_{h\to H}\}
and the rest on
x_{h,\diamond}
. Therefore
x_{h,\diamond}
has mass
at least

p\coloneqq(1-\frac{2H+1}{C_{\mathsf{push}}})
. We have
p>1/2
as long as
C_{\mathsf{push}}\geq 5H
.

Transitions.

At
h=0
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr(\cdot\mid x_{\circ},a)=\begin{cases}\delta_{x_{1,\diamond}}&\text{if}\penalty\ a=0\\
\delta_{x_{1,\star}}&\text{if}\penalty\ a=1\\
\mu_{1}&\text{if}\penalty\ a=\mathfrak{a}_{1}\\
\delta_{\bar{x}_{1\to h^{\prime}}}&\text{if}\penalty\ a=\mathfrak{a}_{h^{\prime}},h^{\prime}\geq 2.\\
\end{cases}

For
h\geq 1
, we have:

•

Boring States:
At the boring state
x_{h,i}
, we always transit to the corresponding boring state in the next layer
x_{h+1,i}
regardless of the action.

•

Highway States:
On the highway state
\bar{x}_{h\to h+1}
, we transit to
\mu_{h+1}
regardless of the action. On highway states
\bar{x}_{h\to h^{\prime}}
for
h^{\prime}>h+1
we transit to
\bar{x}_{h+1,h^{\prime}}
regardless of the action.

•

Special States:
We have

\displaystyle P(\cdot\mid x_{h,\diamond},a)=\begin{cases}\delta_{x_{h+1,H-h}}&\text{if}\penalty\ a=0\\
\delta_{x_{h+1,\star}}&\text{if}\penalty\ a=1,\end{cases}\quad\text{and}\quad P(\cdot\mid x_{h,\star},a)=\begin{cases}\delta_{x_{h+1,0}}&\text{if}\penalty\ a=0\\
\delta_{x_{h+1,H-h+1}}&\text{if}\penalty\ a=1.\end{cases}

Rewards.

All the rewards are at layer
H
:

\displaystyle R(x_{H,\star},0)=0,\quad R(x_{H,\star},1)=C_{\mathsf{push}}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}},\quad\text{and}\quad\forall\penalty\ i\in\{0,1,\cdots,H\}:\penalty\ R(x_{H,i},\cdot)=\frac{C_{\mathsf{push}}^{i}p^{i-1}}{i}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}},

Properties of the Construction.

Now we list several properties of the construction which are more or less immediate to verify.

1.

The state space is of size
O(H^{2})
, the action space is of size
O(H)
, and the policy class is of size
(H+2)\cdot 2^{H}
.

2.

Due to the transitions for the highway states, the distribution
\mu
is admissible at all layers
h\geq 0
.

3.

The minimum probability that
\mu_{h}
places on any state
x\in\mathcal{X}_{h}
is at least
\min\{1/C_{\mathsf{push}},p\}\geq 1/C_{\mathsf{push}}
, so therefore pushforward concentrability is satisfied with parameter
C_{\mathsf{push}}
.

4.

The optimal policy is the all-ones policy,
\pi^{\star}_{h}\equiv 1
for all
h\geq 0
. Therefore
\Pi
is realizable.

B.3.2
Analysis of
PSDP

We will show inductively that
PSDP
returns the all-zeros policy
{\widehat{\pi}}_{h}\equiv 0
for all
h\in[H]
.

•

At layer
H
, the only state for which the value of taking
a_{H}=0
and
a_{H}=1
differ is on
x_{H,\star}
, which is sampled under
\mu_{H}
with probability
1/C_{\mathsf{push}}
. The gap between values is
\En_{x\sim\mu_{H}}\left[r(x,1)-r(x,0)\right]=\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}
, so we set
\mathsf{CB}_{\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}}
to return
{\widehat{\pi}}_{H}\equiv 0
.

•

At layer
H-1
, the two states for which there is a gap in value are the special states
x_{H-1,\diamond}
and
x_{H-1,\star}
. We can compute that

\displaystyle\En_{x\sim\mu_{H-1}}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}_{H}}(x,0)-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}_{H}}(x,1)\right]\geq p\cdot C_{\mathsf{push}}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}-\frac{1}{C_{\mathsf{push}}}\cdot\frac{C_{\mathsf{push}}^{2}p\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}}{2}=\frac{C_{\mathsf{push}}p\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}}{2}>\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}.

Here we use the fact that
\mu_{H-1}(x_{H-1,\diamond})\geq p>1/2
, as well as the assumed lower bound on
C_{\mathsf{push}}
. Therefore,
\mathsf{CB}_{\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}}
must return
{\widehat{\pi}}_{H-1}\equiv 0
.

•

Suppose we are at layer
h
and for all
h^{\prime}>h

PSDP
selects
{\widehat{\pi}}_{h^{\prime}}\equiv 0
. Then the gap in value between action 0 and action 1 is

\displaystyle\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H}}(x,0)-Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}_{h+1:H}}(x,1)\right]

\displaystyle\geq p\cdot\frac{C_{\mathsf{push}}^{H-h}p^{H-h-1}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}}{H-h}-\frac{1}{C_{\mathsf{push}}}\cdot\frac{C_{\mathsf{push}}^{H-h+1}p^{H-h}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}}{H-h+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{C_{\mathsf{push}}^{H-h}p^{H-h}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}}{(H-h)(H-h+1)}>\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}.

The last equality uses the fact that
C_{\mathsf{push}}p\geq 5H/2
.

•

Continuing this way, we can see that for all
h\geq 1
,
PSDP
equipped with
\mathsf{CB}_{\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}}
selects
{\widehat{\pi}}_{h}\equiv 0
. We can calculate that:

\displaystyle Q^{{\widehat{\pi}}_{1:H}}(x_{\circ},a)\begin{cases}=0&\text{if}\penalty\ a=1\\[10.00002pt]
=\frac{C_{\mathsf{push}}^{H-1}p^{H-2}}{H}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}&\text{if}\penalty\ a=0\\[10.00002pt]
\leq\left(\frac{C_{\mathsf{push}}^{H-1}p^{H-2}}{H}+\frac{C_{\mathsf{push}}^{H-1}p^{H-1}}{H-1}\right)\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}\leq\frac{2C_{\mathsf{push}}^{H-1}p^{H-1}}{H-1}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}.&\text{if}\penalty\ a=\mathfrak{a_{i}}\penalty\ \text{for any}\penalty\ i\in[H]\end{cases}

For the last case, we use the rough estimate that
\mu_{h}
places
1/C_{\mathsf{push}}
mass on
x_{h,H}
and the rest elsewhere.

Plugging in the optimal value
V^{\star}
we have that the suboptimality of
PSDP
is at least

\displaystyle V^{\star}-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}\geq\left(\frac{C_{\mathsf{push}}^{H}p^{H-1}}{H}-\frac{2C_{\mathsf{push}}^{H-1}p^{H-1}}{H-1}\right)\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}=(C_{\mathsf{push}})^{\Omega(H)}\varepsilon_{\mathsf{stat}}.

This concludes the proof of
Theorem
8
. ∎

Appendix C
Existence of Emulators Under Pushforward Coverability

A natural question to ask is how to generalize
PLHR
beyond the Block MDP setting. As a starting point for this future research direction, we can show that every pushforward coverable MDP admits a policy emulator with a bounded state space size. We first define pushforward coverability which posits the existence of a good distribution satisfying pushforward concentrability (c.f.
Definition
5
).

Definition 11

(Pushforward Coverability
[
38
,
2
]
)
.

The pushforward coverabilty coefficient for an MDP
M
is

\displaystyle C_{\mathsf{push\_cov}}(M)\coloneqq\max_{h\in[H]}\inf_{\mu_{h}\in\Delta(\mathcal{X}_{h})}\sup_{(x,a,x^{\prime})\in\mathcal{X}_{h-1}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{X}_{h}}\frac{P(x^{\prime}\mid x,a)}{\mu_{h}(x^{\prime})}.

When clear from the context we denote the pushforward concentrability coefficient as
C_{\mathsf{push\_cov}}
.

Proposition 1

(Pushforward Coverable MDPs
\Rightarrow
Small Policy Emulators)
.

Let
M
be an MDP with pushforward coverability coefficient
C_{\mathsf{push\_cov}}
and
\Pi
be any policy class. Then there exists a policy emulator
\widehat{M}
with state space size

\displaystyle\mathrm{poly}\left(C_{\mathsf{push\_cov}},A,H,\varepsilon^{-1},\log\lvert\Pi\rvert,\log\delta^{-1}\right).

A few remarks:

•

Strictly speaking, the policy emulator we construct in
Proposition
1
is not a true MDP, since our construction requires the “transition”
\widehat{P}(\cdot\mid x_{h-1},a_{h-1})
to be an unnormalized measure over the the states in the next layer
\mathcal{X}_{h}[\widehat{M}]
, which may sum to
C_{\mathsf{push\_cov}}\geq 1
. Thus, we slightly abuse the notation for expectation:

\displaystyle\En_{x\sim\widehat{P}(\cdot\mid x_{h-1},a_{h-1})}\left[V^{\pi}(x)\right]\coloneqq\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{h}[\widehat{M}]}\widehat{P}(\cdot\mid x_{h-1},a_{h-1})V^{\pi}(x).

As discussed, the policy emulator anyways is not guaranteed to be a reasonable approximation of the underlying MDP
M
, just an object which enables uniform policy evaluation, so this issue is minor.

•

Lemma 3.1 of
[
2
]
give a result of similar flavor, which shows that pushforward coverable MDPs are approximately
low-rank
. Their proof, however, seems to be quite different. It relies on the Johnson-Lindenstrauss lemma to construct random embeddings which enable approximation of the Bellman backup operator for any arbitrary value function class
\mathcal{F}
.

•

Unfortunately, we do not know how to leverage hybrid resets to construct such a policy emulator in a statistically efficient manner—the naive way to do so requires sample complexity scaling with
C_{\mathsf{span}}(\Pi)
(which could be much larger than
C_{\mathsf{push\_cov}}
). We believe this is an interesting direction for future work.

Proof of
Proposition
1
.

We will prove this by explicitly constructing the policy emulator using the same algorithmic template as in
PLHR
. To construct the state space of the policy emulator, we sample

\widetilde{O}(C_{\mathsf{push}}/\varepsilon^{2})
observations per layer from the distributions
\mu_{1},\cdots,\mu_{H}
, respectively, that witnesses pushforward coverability at every
h\in[H]
. As shown before, the instantaneous rewards
\widehat{R}(x,a)
for every
x\in\mathcal{X}[\widehat{M}]\times\mathcal{A}
of the emulator can be learned via the local simulator up to
\varepsilon
accuracy. Now we show that it is possible to define the transition functions
\widehat{P}(\cdot\mid x,a)
for every
x\in\mathcal{X}[\widehat{M}]\times\mathcal{A}
so that the resulting
\widehat{M}
is an
O(\varepsilon)
-accurate policy emulator for
d_{1}
. We do this inductively:

Claim 1
.

Let
\Gamma_{h}>0
. Suppose that at layer
h\in[H]
:

\displaystyle\forall\penalty\ x\in\mathcal{X}_{h}[\widehat{M}],\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ \pi\in\Pi:\quad\left\lvert V^{\pi}(x)-\widehat{V}^{\pi}(x)\right\rvert\leq\Gamma_{h}.

Then for every
(x_{h-1},a_{h-1})\in\mathcal{X}_{h-1}[\widehat{M}]\times\mathcal{A}
, there exists some
\widehat{P}\in\Delta(\mathcal{X}_{h}[\widehat{M}])
such that

\displaystyle\forall\penalty\ \pi\in\Pi:\quad\left\lvert Q^{\pi}(x_{h-1},a_{h-1})-\widehat{R}(x_{h-1},a_{h-1})-\En_{x\sim\widehat{P}}[\widehat{V}^{\pi}(x)]\right\rvert\leq\Gamma_{h}+2\varepsilon.

Applying this claim backwards from
h=H,\cdots,1
and using the fact that
\Gamma_{H}=\varepsilon
proves
Proposition
1
.

It remains to prove the claim. Let
\widehat{P}
be an unnormalized measure over
\mathcal{X}[\widehat{M}]
(to be defined later). First, we apply the decomposition

\displaystyle\left\lvert Q^{\pi}(x_{h-1},a_{h-1})-\widehat{R}(x_{h-1},a_{h-1})-\En_{x\sim\widehat{P}}[\widehat{V}^{\pi}(x)]\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\penalty\ \left\lvert R(x_{h-1},a_{h-1})-\widehat{R}(x_{h-1},a_{h-1})\right\rvert+\left\lvert\En_{x\sim P}[V^{\pi}(x)]-\En_{x\sim\widehat{P}}\left[V^{\pi}(x)\right]\right\rvert+\left\lvert\En_{x\sim\widehat{P}}[V^{\pi}(x)]-\En_{x\sim\widehat{P}}[\widehat{V}^{\pi}(x)]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\ \Gamma_{h}+\varepsilon+\left\lvert\En_{x\sim P}[V^{\pi}(x)]-\En_{x\sim\widehat{P}}[V^{\pi}(x)]\right\rvert,

(11)

where the last inequality uses the reward estimation accuracy and the assumption in the claim. To control the last term, we apply a change of measure:

\displaystyle\En_{x\sim P}\left[V^{\pi}(x)\right]=\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[\frac{P(x\mid x_{h-1},a_{h-1})}{\mu_{h}(x)}\cdot V^{\pi}(x)\right].

Observe that
\mathcal{X}_{h}[\widehat{M}]=\{x_{h}^{(1)},\cdots,x_{h}^{(n)}\}
are drawn i.i.d. from
\mu_{h}
, and by pushforward coverability, the importance ratio
P(x\mid x_{h-1},a_{h-1})/\mu_{h}(x)\leq C_{\mathsf{push\_cov}}
. Via a standard uniform convergence bound, with probability at least
1-\delta
, for every
\pi\in\Pi

\displaystyle\bigg|\En_{x\sim\mu_{h}}\left[\frac{P(x\mid x_{h-1},a_{h-1})}{\mu_{h}(x)}\cdot V^{\pi}(x)\right]-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\underbrace{\frac{P(x_{h}^{(i)}\mid x_{h-1},a_{h-1})}{n\cdot\mu_{h}(x_{h}^{(i)})}}_{\eqqcolon\widehat{P}(x_{h}^{(i)})}\cdot V^{\pi}(x_{h}^{(i)})\bigg|\leq\varepsilon.

Plugging back our choice of
\widehat{P}
into Eq. (
11
) proves the claim.
∎

Appendix D
Proof of Lower Bounds

In this section, we prove our two main information theoretic lower bounds,
Theorem
2
and
3
.

D.1
Lower Bound Preliminaries

Our lower bounds are facilitated by recent developments that build a unified framework for
interactive statistical decision making
(ISDM)
[
12
]
. We will use an interactive version of Le Cam’s convex hull method, which can be derived as a consequence of
[
12
, Thm. 2]
. For completeness, we include the proof. It closely mirrors the proof of
[
12
, Prop. 4 of]
, which shows how
[
12
, Thm. 2 of]
recovers the noninteractive variant of Le Cam’s convex hull method.

Theorem 9

(Interactive Le Cam’s Convex Hull Method)
.

For parameter space
\Theta
, let
\mathcal{M}=\{M_{\theta}\mid\theta\in\Theta\}
be a class of models indexed by
\Theta
. Let
\mathcal{Y}
be an observation space. For any fixed
\mathsf{Alg}
and distribution
\nu\in\Delta(\Theta)
, let
\Pr^{\nu,\mathsf{Alg}}\in\Delta(\mathcal{Y})
be defined as the distribution over observations when (1) a parameter is drawn
\theta\sim\nu
, (2) the algorithm interacts with model
M_{\theta}
. Let
L:\Theta\times\mathcal{Y}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
be a loss function. Suppose that
\Theta_{0}\subseteq\Theta
and
\Theta_{1}\subseteq\Theta
are subsets that satisfy the separation condition

\displaystyle L(\theta_{0},y)+L(\theta_{1},y)\geq 2\Delta,\quad\forall\penalty\ y\in\mathcal{Y},\theta_{0}\in\Theta_{0},\theta_{1}\in\Theta_{1}.

for some parameter
\Delta>0
. Then it holds that for any
\mathsf{Alg}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\En_{Y\sim\Pr^{M_{\theta},\mathsf{Alg}}}\left[L(\theta,Y)\right]\geq\frac{\Delta}{2}\max_{\nu_{0}\in\Delta(\Theta_{0}),\nu_{1}\in\Delta(\Theta_{1})}\left(1-D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}},\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\right)\right).

(12)

Proof.

We will use
[
12
, Thm. 2]
with total-variational (TV) distance
D_{f}\coloneqq D_{\mathsf{TV}}
. Define the enlarged model class
\overline{\mathcal{M}}\coloneqq\{M_{\nu}:\nu\in\Delta(\Theta)\}
as well as the loss function extension
\overline{L}:\overline{\mathcal{M}}\times\mathcal{Y}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}

\displaystyle\overline{L}(M_{\nu},y)\coloneqq\inf_{\theta\in\mathrm{supp}(\nu)}L(M_{\theta},y).

By the separation condition we have

\displaystyle\overline{L}(M_{\nu_{0}},y)+\overline{L}(M_{\nu_{1}},y)\geq 2\Delta,\quad\forall\penalty\ y\in\mathcal{Y},\nu_{0}\in\Delta(\Theta_{0}),\nu_{1}\in\Delta(\Theta_{1}).

We pick the prior
\mu\coloneqq\mathrm{Unif}(\{M_{\nu_{0}},M_{\nu_{1}}\})
and the reference distribution
\mathbb{Q}\coloneqq\En_{M\sim\mu}[\Pr^{M,\mathsf{Alg}}]
. Observe that

\displaystyle\rho_{\Delta,\mathbb{Q}}\coloneqq\Pr_{M\sim\mu,Y\sim\mathbb{Q}}\left[\overline{L}(M,Y)<\Delta\right]\leq\frac{1}{2}.

Furthermore

\displaystyle\En_{M\sim\mu}\left[D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{M,\mathsf{Alg}},\mathbb{Q}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{{\nu_{0}},\mathsf{Alg}},\mathbb{Q}\right)+\frac{1}{2}D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{{\nu_{1}},\mathsf{Alg}},\mathbb{Q}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{{\nu_{0}},\mathsf{Alg}},\Pr^{{\nu_{1}},\mathsf{Alg}}\right).

Therefore for any
\delta\in[0,\tfrac{1}{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}D_{\mathsf{TV}}(\Pr^{{\nu_{0}},\mathsf{Alg}},\Pr^{{\nu_{1}},\mathsf{Alg}}))
we have

\displaystyle\En_{M\sim\mu}\left[D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{M,\mathsf{Alg}},\mathbb{Q}\right)\right]\leq\begin{cases}D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mathrm{Ber}(1-\delta),\mathrm{Ber}(\rho_{\Delta,\mathbb{Q}})\right)&\text{if}\penalty\ \rho_{\Delta,\mathbb{Q}}\leq 1-\delta\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Therefore
[
12
, Thm. 2]
gives

\displaystyle\En_{\theta\sim\frac{\nu_{0}+\nu_{1}}{2},Y\sim\Pr^{M_{\theta},\mathsf{Alg}}}\left[L(\theta,Y)\right]\geq\En_{M\sim\mu,Y\sim\Pr^{M,\mathsf{Alg}}}\left[\overline{L}(M,Y)\right]\geq\frac{\Delta}{2}\cdot\left(1-D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{{\nu_{0}},\mathsf{Alg}},\Pr^{{\nu_{1}},\mathsf{Alg}}\right)\right).

Taking supremum over
\nu_{0}
and
\nu_{1}
gives the result.
∎

In light of
Theorem
9
, we need to analyze the TV distance between an algorithm
\mathsf{Alg}
interactions with two separate environments given by
\nu_{0}
and
\nu_{1}
. The following chain rule lemma will be useful.

Lemma 4

(Chain Rule for TV Distance, Exercise I.43 of
[
42
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be any observation space, let
\mathbb{P}^{Z_{n}}
and
\mathbb{Q}^{Z_{n}}
be distributions over
n
-tuples of
\mathcal{Z}
. Then

\displaystyle D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mathbb{P}^{Z_{n}},\mathbb{Q}^{Z_{n}}\right)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\En_{Z_{1:i-1}\sim\mathbb{P}^{Z_{n}}}\left[D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mathbb{P}\left[Z_{i}\mid Z_{1:i-1}\right],\mathbb{Q}\left[Z_{i}\mid Z_{1:i-1}\right]\right)\right].

Additional Notation.

We use
\mathsf{Alg}
to denote a deterministic algorithm that collects
T
samples, i.e., full-length episodes (from either the generative model or
\mu
-reset access). For any
t\in[T]
we define
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
to be the sigma-field of everything observed in the first
t-1
episodes. We further define for any
h\in[H]
the filtration
\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}
to be the sigma-field of everything observed in the first
t-1
episodes as well as the first
h-1
steps of the
t
-th sample. To handle the difference in interaction models,
\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}
is defined slightly differently:

•

For the generative model,
\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\coloneqq\sigma(\mathcal{F}_{t-1},\{(X_{t,i},A_{t,i},R_{t,i},X^{\prime}_{t,i})\}_{i\leq h-1})
, where
R_{t,i}
and
X^{\prime}_{t,i}
are the reward and transition which is returned by the environment. The tuple
(X_{t,h},A_{t,h})
is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}
(since
\mathsf{Alg}
is deterministic).

•

For the
\mu
-reset model,
\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}=\sigma(\mathcal{F}_{t-1},\{(X_{t,i},A_{t,i},R_{t,i})\}_{h_{\bot}\leq i\leq h-1})
. Here,
h_{\bot}
is the starting layer of episode
t
, which is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
; furthermore, the action
A_{t,h}
is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\cup\{X_{t,h}\}
(since
\mathsf{Alg}
is deterministic).

Lastly, we denote partial policies
\pi_{h_{\bot}:h_{\top}}\in\Pi_{h_{\bot}:h_{\top}}
for some
1\leq h_{\bot}\leq h_{\top}\leq H
. We sometimes drop the subscript
h_{\bot}:h_{\top}
if clear from context. We may also overload equality to compare partial policies
\pi_{h_{\bot}:h_{\top}}
with complete policies
\pi^{\prime}_{1:H}
, i.e., we write
\pi=\pi^{\prime}
iff
\pi_{h_{\bot}:h_{\top}}=\pi^{\prime}_{h_{\bot}:h_{\top}}
.

D.2
Proof of
Theorem
2

The construction of
Theorem
2
is given by the rich observation combination lock, which has appeared in previous lower bounds for RL
[
46
,
24
]
. Since the rich observation combination lock is a Block MDP with
2
latent states per layer, it satisfies
C_{\mathsf{cov}}=2
. The key intuition is that the set of observations associated with latent states on the good chain is much smaller than the set of observations associated with latent states on the bad chain. Therefore, even though coverability is small, the learner cannot effectively use the generative model to “guess” observations which are emitted from states on the good chain. In other words, they cannot sample from a distribution with low concentrability, which is crucial for learning
\pi^{\star}
.

Lower Bound Construction.

First, we define the policy class
\Pi
to be open loop policies:

\displaystyle\Pi\coloneqq\{\pi:\forall x\in\mathcal{X}_{h},\pi_{h}(x)\equiv a_{h},(a_{1},\cdots,a_{H})\in\mathcal{A}^{H}\}.

We define a family of Block MDPs
\mathcal{M}=\{M_{\pi^{\star},\phi}\}_{\pi^{\star}\in\Pi,\phi\in\Phi}
which are parameterized by an optimal policy
\pi^{\star}\in\Pi
and a decoding function
\phi\in\Phi
(to be described). An example is illustrated in
Figure
2
.

•

Latent MDP:
The latent state space
\mathcal{S}
is layered where each
\mathcal{S}_{h}\coloneqq\{s_{h}^{\mathsf{g}},s_{h}^{\mathsf{b}}\}
is comprised of a good and bad state. We abbreviate the state as
\{\mathsf{g},\mathsf{b}\}
if the layer
h
is clear from context. The action space
\mathcal{A}=\{0,1\}
. The starting state is always
\mathsf{g}
. Let
\pi^{\star}\in\Pi
be any policy, which can be represented by a vector in
(\pi^{\star}_{1},\cdots,\pi^{\star}_{H})\in\{0,1\}^{H}
. The latent transitions/rewards of an MDP parameterized by
\pi^{\star}\in\Pi
are given by the standard combination lock. For every
h\in[H]
:

\displaystyle P_{\mathsf{lat}}(\cdot\mid s,a)=\begin{cases}\delta_{s_{h+1}^{\mathsf{g}}}&\text{if}\penalty\ s=s_{h}^{\mathsf{g}}\penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ a=\pi^{\star}_{h}\\
\delta_{s_{h+1}^{\mathsf{b}}}&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}\quad\text{and}\quad R_{\mathsf{lat}}(s,a)=\mathbbm{1}\left\{s=s_{H}^{\mathsf{g}},a=\pi^{\star}_{H}\right\}.

•

Rich Observations:
The observation state space
\mathcal{X}
is layered where each
\mathcal{X}_{h}\coloneqq\{x_{h}^{(1)},\cdots,x_{h}^{(m)}\}
with
m=2^{2H}
. The decoding function class
\Phi
is the collection of all decoders which for every
h\geq 2
assigns
s_{h}^{\mathsf{g}}
to a subset of
\mathcal{X}_{h}
of size
2^{H}
and
s_{h}^{\mathsf{b}}
to the rest:

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{\phi:\mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathcal{S}:\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ x_{1}\in\mathcal{X}_{1},\penalty\ \phi(x_{1})=\mathsf{g},\text{ and }\forall\penalty\ h\geq 2,\left\lvert\left\{x\in\mathcal{X}_{h}:\phi(x)=\mathsf{g}\right\}\right\rvert=2^{H}\right\},

\displaystyle\text{so that}\penalty\ \lvert\Phi\rvert={2^{2H}\choose 2^{H}}^{H-1}=2^{2^{\widetilde{O}(H)}}.

In the MDP parameterized by
\phi\in\Phi
, the emission for every
s\in\mathcal{S}
is
\psi(s)=\mathrm{Unif}\left(\left\{x\in\mathcal{X}_{h}:\phi(x)=s\right\}\right)
.

Now we establish several facts about the lower bound construction. Fix any
M=M_{\pi^{\star},\phi}
.

1.

Since
M
is a Block MDP with 2 latent states per layer,
C_{\mathsf{cov}}(\Pi,M)=2
.

2.

The class
\Pi
satisfies policy completeness with respect to
M
. To see this, fix any layer
h\in[H]
and partial policy
\pi\in\Pi_{h+1:H}
. We have:

\displaystyle\forall\penalty\ (x,a)\in\mathcal{X}_{h}\times\mathcal{A}:\quad Q^{\pi}(x,a)=\mathbbm{1}\left\{\phi(x)=s_{h}^{\mathsf{g}},a=\pi^{\star}_{h},\pi=\pi^{\star}_{h+1:H}\right\}.

Therefore in
Definition
2
we can take
\widetilde{\pi}_{h}\coloneqq\pi^{\star}_{h}
, which satisfies
\widetilde{\pi}_{h}\in\argmax_{a\in\mathcal{A}}Q^{\pi}(x,a)
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}_{h}
.

Sample Complexity Lower Bound.

We will use
Theorem
9
to prove our lower bound. First we need to instantiate the parameter space. We will let
\Theta\coloneqq\{(\pi^{\star},\phi):\penalty\ \pi^{\star}\in\Pi,\phi\in\Phi\}
so that
\mathcal{M}=\{M_{\theta}\}_{\theta\in\Theta}=\{M_{\pi^{\star},\phi}\}_{\pi^{\star}\in\Pi,\phi\in\Phi}
. We further denote the subsets

\displaystyle\Theta_{0}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{(\pi^{\star},\phi):\penalty\ \pi^{\star}\in\Pi\penalty\ \text{s.t.}\penalty\ \pi^{\star}_{H}=0,\phi\in\Phi\}

\displaystyle\Theta_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{(\pi^{\star},\phi):\penalty\ \pi^{\star}\in\Pi\penalty\ \text{s.t.}\penalty\ \pi^{\star}_{H}=1,\phi\in\Phi\}

The observation space
\mathcal{Y}
is defined as the set of observations over
T
rounds as well as returned proper policy for an algorithm interacting with the MDP, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}\coloneqq(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{A}\times[0,1])^{HT}\times\Pi.

For technical convenience, we will suppose that
\mathsf{Alg}
sequentially queries the generative model by looping over layers, i.e., it queries
(X_{1},A_{1})\in\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\mathcal{A}
, then
(X_{2},A_{2})\in\mathcal{X}_{2}\times\mathcal{A}
, etc. This only increases the sample complexity of
\mathsf{Alg}
by a factor of
H
, which is negligible since we will show that
\mathsf{Alg}
requires
\exp(H)
samples.

For an observation
y\in\mathcal{Y}
we define the final returned policy as
y^{\pi}
. The loss function is given by

\displaystyle L((\pi^{\star},\phi),y)\coloneqq\mathbbm{1}\left\{\pi^{\star}\neq y^{\pi}\right\}.

Then we have for any
y\in\mathcal{Y}
,
(\pi^{\star}_{0},\phi_{0})\in\Theta_{0}
, and
(\pi^{\star}_{1},\phi_{1})\in\Theta_{1}
that

\displaystyle L((\pi^{\star}_{0},\phi_{0}),y)+L((\pi^{\star}_{1},\phi_{1}),y)\geq 1\coloneqq 2\Delta,

since the last bit of
y^{\pi}
can be either 0 or 1, thus only matching exactly one of
\pi^{\star}_{0}
and
\pi^{\star}_{1}
.

Now we are ready to apply
Theorem
9
. We get that for any
\mathsf{Alg}
, we must have

\displaystyle\sup_{(\pi^{\star},\phi)\in\Pi\times\Phi}\En_{Y\sim\Pr^{M_{\pi^{\star},\phi},\mathsf{Alg}}}\left[V^{\star}-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sup_{(\pi^{\star},\phi)\in\Pi\times\Phi}\En_{Y\sim\Pr^{M_{\pi^{\star},\phi},\mathsf{Alg}}}\left[1-\mathbbm{1}\left\{\pi^{\star}=Y^{\pi}\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=\sup_{(\pi^{\star},\phi)\in\Pi\times\Phi}\En_{Y\sim\Pr^{M_{\pi^{\star},\phi},\mathsf{Alg}}}\left[L((\pi^{\star},\phi),Y)\right]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{4}\cdot\max_{\nu_{0}\in\Delta(\Theta_{0}),\nu_{1}\in\Delta(\Theta_{1})}\left(1-D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}},\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{4}\cdot\left(1-D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{\mathrm{Unif}(\Theta_{0}),\mathsf{Alg}},\Pr^{\mathrm{Unif}(\Theta_{1}),\mathsf{Alg}}\right)\right).

It remains to compute an upper bound
D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{\mathrm{Unif}(\Theta_{0}),\mathsf{Alg}},\Pr^{\mathrm{Unif}(\Theta_{1}),\mathsf{Alg}}\right)
which holds for any
\mathsf{Alg}
. This is accomplished by the following lemma.

Lemma 5
.

Let
T=2^{O(H)}
. For any deterministic
\mathsf{Alg}
that adaptively collects
HT
samples via generative access, we have

\displaystyle D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{\mathrm{Unif}(\Theta_{0}),\mathsf{Alg}},\Pr^{\mathrm{Unif}(\Theta_{1}),\mathsf{Alg}}\right)\leq\frac{T^{4}H}{2^{H-9}}.

Plugging in
Lemma
5
, we conclude that for any
\mathsf{Alg}
that collects
2^{cH}
samples for sufficiently small constant
c>0
must be
1/8
-suboptimal in expectation. This concludes the proof of
Theorem
2
.∎

D.3
Proof of
Lemma
5
(TV Distance Calculation for
Theorem
2
)

Let us denote
\nu_{0}\coloneqq\mathrm{Unif}(\Theta_{0})
and
\nu_{1}\coloneqq\mathrm{Unif}(\Theta_{1})
. By the TV distance chain rule (
Lemma
4
) we have

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003ptD_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}},\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h},A_{t,h}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right],\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h},A_{t,h}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right]\right)\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h}^{\prime},R_{t,h}\mid X_{t,h},A_{t,h},\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right],\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h}^{\prime},R_{t,h}\mid X_{t,h},A_{t,h},\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right]\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h}^{\prime},R_{t,h}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right],\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h}^{\prime},R_{t,h}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right]\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{h=1}^{H-1}\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h}^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right],\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h}^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right]\right)\right]}_{\text{transition TV distance}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\underbrace{\sum_{t=1}^{T}\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right],\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]\right)\right]}_{\text{reward TV distance}}.

The first equality follows from the fact that the TV distance for the distribution over state-action pairs
(X_{t,h},A_{t,h})
is zero since
(X_{t,h},A_{t,h})
is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}
. The second equality follows because the rewards only come at the last layer in every MDP instance.

We now show how to bound each term separately.

Transition TV Distance.

For the transition TV distance, we have the following computation for all
t\in[T],h\in[H-1]
:

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h}^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right],\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h}^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right]\right)\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{\leq}\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h}^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right],\mathrm{Unif}(\mathcal{X}_{h+1})\right)\right]+\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h}^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right],\mathrm{Unif}(\mathcal{X}_{h+1})\right)\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(ii)}{\leq}\frac{t}{2^{H-3}}.

(13)

The inequality
(i)
follows by triangle inequality and the inequality
(ii)
uses
Lemma
6
.

Reward TV Distance.

We can compute that

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right],\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]\right)\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{\leq}\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right],\delta_{0}\right)\right]+\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right],\delta_{0}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(ii)}{=}\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]\right]+\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]\right]\overset{(iii)}{\leq}t\cdot\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H-8}}.

(14)

The inequality
(i)
follows by triangle inequality, while
(ii)
uses the fact that the rewards are in
\{0,1\}
. Lastly,
(iii)
follows by
Lemma
7
.

Final Bound.

Thus, combining Eqs. (
13
) and (
14
) we can conclude that:

\displaystyle D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}},\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\right)\leq\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H-3}}+\frac{T^{4}H}{2^{H-8}}\leq\frac{T^{4}H}{2^{H-9}}.

This concludes the proof of
Lemma
5
.∎

Lemma 6

(Transition TV Distance for Construction in
Theorem
2
)
.

For any
t\in[T],h\in[H-1]
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h}^{\prime}\mid X_{t,h},A_{t,h},\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right]-\mathrm{Unif}(\mathcal{X}_{h+1})\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{t}{2^{H-2}},

\displaystyle\left\|\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h}^{\prime}\mid X_{t,h},A_{t,h},\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right]-\mathrm{Unif}(\mathcal{X}_{h+1})\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{t}{2^{H-2}}.

Proof of
Lemma
6
.

We prove the bound for
\nu_{0}
, since the proof for
\nu_{1}
is identical. Denote the “annotated” sigma-field

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}^{\prime}=\sigma\Bigg(

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1},X_{t,h},A_{t,h},\left\{\phi(X):X\in\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\cup\{X_{t,h}\}\right\},

\displaystyle\left\{\mathbbm{1}\left\{A=\pi^{\star}(X)\right\}:(X,A)\in\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\cup\{X_{t,h},A_{t,h}\}\right\}\Bigg)

to be the sigma-field which also includes the latent state labels for all of the seen observations as well as whether the actions taken followed
\pi^{\star}
or not. Let us denote
\ell=\phi(X_{t,h}^{\prime})\in\{\mathsf{g},\mathsf{b}\}
to be the latent state of the next observation. Observe that the label
\ell
is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}^{\prime}
since the filtration
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}^{\prime}
includes
\phi(X_{t,h})
as well as
\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{t,h}=\pi^{\star}(X_{t,h})\right\}
. Furthermore denote
\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}
to denote the total number of observations that we have encountered already in layer
h+1
and
\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\ell}
to denote the observations we have encountered whose latent state is
\ell
.

Under the uniform distribution over decoders, the assignment of the remaining observations is equally likely. Therefore we can write the distribution of
X_{t,h}^{\prime}
as:

\displaystyle\text{if}\penalty\ \ell=\mathsf{g}:

\displaystyle\quad\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h}^{\prime}=x\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}^{\prime}\right]=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2^{H}}&\text{if}\penalty\ x\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\ell}\\
0&\text{if}\penalty\ x\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}-\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\ell}\\
\frac{1}{2^{H}}\cdot\frac{2^{H}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\ell}\rvert}{2^{2H}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}\rvert}&\text{if}\penalty\ x\in\mathcal{X}_{h+1}-\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\text{if}\penalty\ \ell=\mathsf{b}:

\displaystyle\quad\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h}^{\prime}=x\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}^{\prime}\right]=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2^{2H}-2^{H}}&\text{if}\penalty\ x\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\ell}\\
0&\text{if}\penalty\ x\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}-\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\ell}\\
\frac{1}{2^{2H}-2^{H}}\cdot\frac{2^{2H}-2^{H}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\ell}\rvert}{2^{2H}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}\rvert}&\text{if}\penalty\ x\in\mathcal{X}_{h+1}-\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}\end{cases}

We elaborate on the calculation for the last probability in each case. Suppose
\ell=\mathsf{g}
. Then for any
x\in\mathcal{X}_{h+1}-\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}
which has not been observed yet we assign
\phi(x)=\ell
in

\displaystyle{2^{2H}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}\rvert-1\choose 2^{H}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\ell}\rvert-1}\text{ ways out of }{2^{2H}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}\rvert\choose 2^{H}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\ell}\rvert}\text{ assignments.}

\displaystyle\quad\Longrightarrow\phi(x)=\mathsf{g}\text{ with probability }\frac{2^{H}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\ell}\rvert}{2^{2H}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}\rvert}.

For each assignment where
\phi(x)=\mathsf{g}
we will select it with probability
1/2^{H}
since the emission is uniform, giving us the final probability as claimed. A similar calculation can be done for the case where
\ell=\mathsf{b}
.

Therefore we can calculate the final bound that

\displaystyle\left\|\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h}^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}^{\prime}\right]-\mathrm{Unif}(\mathcal{X}_{h+1})\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{h+1}}\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h}^{\prime}=x\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}^{\prime}\right]-\frac{1}{2^{2H}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\begin{cases}\frac{\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\mathsf{g}}\rvert}{2^{H}}+\frac{\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\mathsf{b}}\rvert}{2^{2H}}+\left\lvert\frac{2^{H}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\mathsf{g}}\rvert}{2^{H}}-\frac{2^{2H}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}\rvert}{2^{2H}}\right\rvert&\text{if}\penalty\ \ell=\mathsf{g},\\[5.0pt]
\frac{\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\mathsf{b}}\rvert}{2^{2H}-2^{H}}+\frac{\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\mathsf{g}}\rvert}{2^{2H}}+\left\lvert\frac{2^{2H}-2^{H}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\mathsf{b}}\rvert}{2^{2H}-2^{H}}-\frac{2^{2H}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}\rvert}{2^{2H}}\right\rvert&\text{if}\penalty\ \ell=\mathsf{b}.\end{cases}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4\cdot\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}\rvert}{2^{H}}\leq\frac{4t}{2^{H}}.

Since
\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h}^{\prime}\mid X_{t,h},A_{t,h},\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right]=\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}[X_{t,h}^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}^{\prime}]
, we have by convexity of TV distance and Jensen’s inequality,

\displaystyle\left\|\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h}^{\prime}\mid X_{t,h},A_{t,h},\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right]-\mathrm{Unif}(\mathcal{X}_{h+1})\right\|_{1}\leq\frac{4t}{2^{H}},

which concludes the proof of
Lemma
6
.
∎

Lemma 7

(Reward Bound for Construction in
Theorem
2
)
.

Let
T\leq 2^{H}
. For any
t\in[T]
:

\displaystyle\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\leq t\cdot\frac{HT^{2}}{2^{H-7}}.

\displaystyle\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\leq t\cdot\frac{HT^{2}}{2^{H-7}}.

Proof of
Lemma
7
.

To show the proof, we use induction to show that the probability of see nonzero reward remains small throughout the entire execution of
\mathsf{Alg}
.

Peeling Off Bad Events.

First, we will peel off a couple “bad” events which occur with low probability:

•

Let
\mathcal{E}_{F}
be the event that every freshly sampled observation (i.e., querying the generative model on some observation
X_{t,h}\notin\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}
) in any layer
h\geq 2
has a bad label:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{F}\coloneqq\left\{\phi(X_{t,h})=\mathsf{b}\text{ for every }t\in[T],h\geq 2,X_{t,h}\notin\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right\}.

We will show in
Lemma
8
that due to the unbalanced sizes of every layer and the uniform distribution over decoders,
\mathcal{E}_{F}
must occur with high probability. This captures the intuition that the generative model affords no additional power over local simulation, since data generated from states with a bad label are not informative, and with high probability all fresh samples have a bad label.

•

Let
\mathcal{E}_{N}
be the event that every sampled transition is a new observation that has never been seen before:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{N}\coloneq\left\{X_{t,h}^{\prime}\notin\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\text{ for every }t\in[T],h\in[H]\right\}.

We show in
Lemma
9
that due to the large state space in every layer,
\mathcal{E}_{N}
also occurs with high probability, therefore capturing the intuition that transitions are not informative for learning the optimal policy
\pi^{\star}
.

We can compute that:

\displaystyle\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{F}^{c}\right]+\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{N}^{c}\right]+\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{HT\cdot 2^{H}}{2^{2H}-2T}+\frac{HT^{2}}{2^{H}}+\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]\right].

\displaystyle\leq\frac{HT^{2}}{2^{H-2}}+\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]\right],

(15)

where the second line uses
Lemma
8
and
Lemma
9
, and the last line uses the fact that
T=2^{O(H)}
.

We will show inductively that under the distribution
\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}
, rewards are nonzero with exponentially small (in
H
) probability. Then we use this bound to prove the final guarantee.

Inductive Claim.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{R,t}
be the event that after the
t
-th episode, all of the observed rewards are zero, i.e.,
\mathcal{E}_{R,t}\coloneqq\{R_{t^{\prime},H}=0\text{ for all }t^{\prime}\leq t\}
. We claim that

\displaystyle\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{R,t}^{c}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right]\leq t\cdot\frac{HT^{2}}{2^{H-7}}.

(16)

We show this via induction. The base case of
t=0
trivially holds. Now suppose that
Claim
16
holds for at episode
t-1
. We show that it holds at episode
t
. We calculate that

\displaystyle\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{R,t}^{c}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{\leq}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}^{c}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right]+\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(ii)}{\leq}(t-1)\cdot\frac{HT^{2}}{2^{H-7}}+\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]\right]

(17)

Here, inequality
(i)
uses the fact that if we see zero reward in the first
t-1
episodes,
\mathcal{E}_{R,t}^{c}
can only happen if
R_{t,H}=1
; inequality
(ii)
uses the inductive hypothesis.

Now we will provide a bound on the reward distribution. We can calculate that

\displaystyle\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\phi\in\Phi}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\phi,\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{=}\sum_{\phi\in\Phi}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\phi(X_{t,H})=\mathsf{g}\text{ and }A_{t,H}=0\right\}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\phi\in\Phi}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\phi(X_{t,H})=\mathsf{g}\right\}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]=\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(X_{t,H})=\mathsf{g}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right].

(18)

For
(i)
we use the fact that the event
R_{t,H}=1
is measurable with respect to
\phi
and
\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}
.

Dataset as a DAG.

To further bound Eq. (
18
), we take the following viewpoint: for any
t\in[T],h\in[H]
, the collected dataset
\mathcal{F}_{t,h}
can be viewed as directed acyclic graph (DAG) with set of vertices given by the observations in
\mathcal{F}_{t,h}
. In this DAG, the edges are labeled with
A\in\{0,1\}
, and we draw an edge
X\to X^{\prime}
with label
a
if the sample
(X,A,X^{\prime})
exists in the dataset
\mathcal{F}_{t,h}
. For any observation
x\in\mathcal{X}
and filtration
\mathcal{F}
, we define the root-layer operation
\mathsf{RootLayer}(x\mid\mathcal{F})
to be minimum layer
h
for which there exists some path in the DAG representation of
\mathcal{F}
from some
X_{h}\to x
with
X_{h}\in\mathcal{F}
. If
x\notin\mathcal{F}
, we have the convention that
\mathsf{RootLayer}(x\mid\mathcal{F})=h(x)
. We also denote
\mathsf{Root}(x\mid\mathcal{F})
to be any observation
X_{h}\in\mathcal{F}\cup\{x\}
which witnesses
\mathsf{RootLayer}(x\mid\mathcal{F})=h
.

We can further calculate that

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(X_{t,H})=\mathsf{g}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\leq\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\begin{subarray}{c}\text{exists a path $X_{1}\to X_{t,H}$ in $\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}$}\\
\text{labeled by $\pi^{\star}_{1:H-1}$ }\end{subarray}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]\right].

(19)

The inequality is shown as follows: if
\mathsf{RootLayer}(X_{t,H}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1})\geq 2
, then event
\mathcal{E}_{F}
guarantees that any observation
X_{h}\in\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}
which witnesses the value of
\mathsf{RootLayer}
has a bad label
\phi(X_{h})=\mathsf{b}
, so therefore we must also have
\phi(X_{t,H})=\mathsf{b}
. Otherwise, if
