\mathsf{RootLayer}(X_{t,H}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1})=1
, then
\phi(X_{t,H})=\mathsf{g}
implies that the path
X_{1}\to X_{t,H}
which witnesses
\mathsf{RootLayer}=1
must be labeled by
\pi^{\star}_{1:H-1}
.

Analyzing the Posterior of
\pi^{\star}
.

To bound Eq. (
19
), we apply chain rule and a change of measure argument.

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\begin{subarray}{c}\text{exists a path $X_{1}\to X_{t,H}$ in $\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}$}\\
\text{labeled by $\pi^{\star}_{1:H-1}$ }\end{subarray}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\begin{subarray}{c}\text{exists a path $X_{1}\to X_{t,H}$ in $\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}$}\\
\text{labeled by $\pi$ }\end{subarray}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]\cdot\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\pi^{\star}=\pi\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{HT^{2}}{2^{H-6}}+\frac{1}{\lvert\Pi_{1:H-1}\rvert}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\begin{subarray}{c}\text{exists a path $X_{1}\to X_{t,H}$ in $\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}$}\\
\text{labeled by $\pi$ }\end{subarray}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]\leq\frac{HT^{2}}{2^{H-7}}.

(20)

The first inequality follows by the calculation in
Lemma
10
, and the second inequality follows because there are at most
T
paths in the DAG representation of
\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}
.

Completing Induction for
Claim
16
.

By combining Eqs. (
17
)–(
20
) we see that as long as
T\leq 2^{O(H)}
, then

\displaystyle\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{R,t}^{c}\right]\leq(t-1)\cdot\frac{HT^{2}}{2^{H-7}}+\frac{HT^{2}}{2^{H-7}}=t\cdot\frac{HT^{2}}{2^{H-7}}.

This proves the claim.

Final Bounds for
Lemma
7
.

To prove the first inequality, we have directly by
Claim
16

\displaystyle\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]\right]\leq\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{R,t}^{c}\right]\leq t\cdot\frac{HT^{2}}{2^{H-7}}.

To prove the second inequality in the lemma statement, we can get a similar bound as Eq. (
15
):

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{HT^{2}}{2^{H-2}}+\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\cdot\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{HT^{2}}{2^{H-2}}+\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}^{c}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right]+\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}^{c}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\cdot\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{HT^{2}}{2^{H-2}}+(t-1)\frac{HT^{2}}{2^{H-5}}+\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}^{c}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\cdot\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]\right],

and from here one can replicate the above argument to get a bound on this quantity. The details are omitted. This concludes the proof of
Lemma
7
.
∎

Lemma 8
.

\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}[\mathcal{E}_{F}^{c}]\leq\frac{HT\cdot 2^{H}}{2^{2H}-2T}
.

Proof.

Let us consider the set
\mathcal{I}
(which is a random variable that depends on the interaction of
\mathsf{Alg}
with
\nu_{0}
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}=\{(t,h):X_{t,h}\notin\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\}.

We have

\displaystyle\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{F}^{c}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{h=2}^{H}\mathbbm{1}\left\{(t,h)\in\mathcal{I}\text{ and }\phi(X_{t,h})=\mathsf{g}\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{h=2}^{H}\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\Pr\left[(t,h)\in\mathcal{I}\text{ and }\phi(X_{t,h})=\mathsf{g}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{h=2}^{H}\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\Pr\left[\phi(X_{t,h})=\mathsf{g}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1},(t,h)\in\mathcal{I}\right]\right].

(21)

Now we will bound the quantity
\Pr\left[\phi(X_{t,h})=\mathsf{g}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1},(t,h)\in\mathcal{I}\right]
for any
t\in[T]
,
h\geq 2
. Consider the annotated filtration

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}^{\prime}\coloneqq\sigma\left(\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1},\left\{\phi(X):X\in\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right\}\right)

which includes the decoder label for all observations seen thus far. We compute that for any
t\in[T],h\geq 2
:

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\phi(X_{t,h})=\mathsf{g}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}^{\prime},(t,h)\in\mathcal{I}\right]=\frac{2^{H}-\lvert\{X\in\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}:\phi(X)=\mathsf{g}\}\rvert}{2^{2H}-2t+1},

(22)

since once we have fixed the value of the decoder on the
2t-1
seen examples at layer
h
, the label of a new state is uniform over all remaining possibilities.

Continuing the calcuation from Eq. (
21
):

\displaystyle\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{F}^{c}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{h=2}^{H}\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\Pr\left[\phi(X_{t,h})=\mathsf{g}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1},(t,h)\in\mathcal{I}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{h=2}^{H}\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\En\left[\Pr\left[\phi(X_{t,h})=\mathsf{g}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}^{\prime},(t,h)\in\mathcal{I}\right]\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1},(t,h)\in\mathcal{I}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{h=2}^{H}\frac{2^{H}}{2^{2H}-2T}\leq\frac{HT\cdot 2^{H}}{2^{2H}-2T}.

The second inequality uses Eq.
(
22
)
. This completes the proof of
Lemma
8
.
∎

Lemma 9
.

\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}[\mathcal{E}_{N}^{c}]\leq\frac{HT^{2}}{2^{H}}
.

Proof.

Any sampled transition
X_{t,h}^{\prime}
has probability at most
T/2^{H}
of being a repeated state (which is maximized if
X_{t,h}^{\prime}
has a good label and we have already sampled
T
such observations from that given latent). Applying union bound over
T(H-1)
transition samples gives us the final bound.
∎

Lemma 10

(Posterior of
\pi^{\star}
)
.

Fix any
t\in[T]
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\cdot\left\|\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\pi^{\star}=\cdot\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]-\mathrm{Unif}(\Pi_{1:H-1})\right\|_{1}\leq\frac{HT^{2}}{2^{H-6}}.

Proof.

In what follows all of the probabilities are taken with respect to
\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}
. We can compute that

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\cdot\left\|\Pr\left[\pi^{\star}=\cdot\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]-\mathrm{Unif}(\Pi_{1:H-1})\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\cdot\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}}\left\lvert\Pr\left[\pi^{\star}=\pi\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]-\frac{1}{2^{H-1}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\cdot 2\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}}\left[\Pr\left[\pi^{\star}=\pi\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]-\frac{1}{2^{H-1}}\right]_{+}

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\cdot\frac{2}{2^{H-1}}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}}\left[\frac{\Pr\left[\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]}{\Pr\left[\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]}-1\right]_{+}

\displaystyle\leq 2\max_{\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}}\left[\frac{\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\cdot\Pr\left[\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]}{\Pr\left[\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]}-1\right]_{+}.

(23)

Now we will provide explicit calculations for the conditional distribution of
\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}
for every choice of optimal policy
\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}
. Fix any
\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}
such that
R_{i,H}=0
for all
i\in[t-1]
and no repeated transitions (otherwise we can trivially upper bound Eq. (
23
) by 0). By chain rule we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]=\left(\prod_{i=1}^{t}\prod_{h=1}^{H-1}\Pr\left[X^{\prime}_{i,h}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,h-1}\right]\right)\times\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\Pr\left[R_{i,H}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,H-1}\right].

We bound the transition and reward probabilities separately using
Claim
2
and
Claim
3
.

Claim 2
.

Fix any
i\in[t]
and
h\in[H-1]
. We have for every
\pi\in\Pi
:

\displaystyle\Pr\left[X^{\prime}_{i,h}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,h-1}\right]\in\frac{1}{2^{2H}}\cdot\left[\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right),\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)\right].

To prove this claim, we can compute that

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\Pr\left[X^{\prime}_{i,h}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,h-1}\right]=\sum_{\ell\in\{\mathsf{g},\mathsf{b}\}}\Pr\left[X^{\prime}_{i,h}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,h-1},\phi_{h}(X_{i,h})=\ell\right]\Pr\left[\phi_{h}(X_{i,h})=\ell\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,h-1}\right]

Case 1: if
A_{i,h}=\pi^{\star}_{h}
.
If we started in a good state then we would transition to the good state, so

\displaystyle\Pr\left[X^{\prime}_{i,h}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,h-1}\right]

\displaystyle=\Pr\left[\phi_{h}(X_{i,h})=\mathsf{g}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,h-1}\right]\cdot\frac{\Pr\left[\phi_{h+1}(X_{i,h}^{\prime})=\mathsf{g}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,h-1},\phi_{h}(X_{i,h})=\mathsf{g}\right]}{2^{2H}-2^{H}}

\displaystyle\quad+\Pr\left[\phi_{h}(X_{i,h})=\mathsf{b}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,h-1}\right]\cdot\frac{\Pr\left[\phi_{h+1}(X_{i,h}^{\prime})=\mathsf{b}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,h-1},\phi_{h}(X_{i,h})=\mathsf{b}\right]}{2^{H}}

Case 2: if
A_{i,h}\neq\pi^{\star}_{h}
.
In this case we know that regardless of the label of
X_{i,h}
we transition to a bad state, so

\displaystyle\Pr\left[X^{\prime}_{i,h}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,h-1}\right]

\displaystyle=\Pr\left[\phi_{h}(X_{i,h})=\mathsf{g}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,h-1}\right]\cdot\frac{\Pr\left[\phi_{h+1}(X_{i,h}^{\prime})=\mathsf{b}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,h-1},\phi_{h}(X_{i,h})=\mathsf{g}\right]}{2^{2H}-2^{H}}

\displaystyle\quad+\Pr\left[\phi_{h}(X_{i,h})=\mathsf{b}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,h-1}\right]\cdot\frac{\Pr\left[\phi_{h+1}(X_{i,h}^{\prime})=\mathsf{b}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,h-1},\phi_{h}(X_{i,h})=\mathsf{b}\right]}{2^{2H}-2^{H}}

Either way, applying
Lemma
11
concludes the proof of
Claim
2
.

Claim 3
.

Fix any
i\in[t-1]
. We have for every
\pi\in\Pi
:

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{F}\right\}\Pr\left[R_{i,H}=1\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,H-1}\right]\leq\mathbbm{1}\left\{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{exists a path $X_{1}\to X_{H}$ in $\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}$}\\
\text{labeled by $\pi$ }\end{subarray}\right\}.

To prove this claim, we use casework.

Case 1: if
\mathsf{RootLayer}(X_{i,H}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i,H-1})\geq 2
.
Then we must have

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{F}\right\}\cdot\Pr\left[R_{i,H}=1\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,H-1}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{F}\right\}\Pr\left[\phi(\mathsf{Root}(X_{i,H}))=\mathsf{g}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,H-1}\right]=0.

The equality holds because
\mathcal{E}_{F}\Rightarrow\{\phi(\mathsf{Root}(X_{i,H}))=\mathsf{b}\}
. This proves
Claim
3
in this case.

Case 2: if
\mathsf{RootLayer}(X_{i,h}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i,H-1})=1
.
In this case we can compare the path witnessing
\mathsf{RootLayer}=1
with the labeling
\pi^{\star}
, and we get

\displaystyle\Pr\left[R_{i,H}=1\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i,H-1}\right]\leq\mathbbm{1}\left\{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{exists a path $X_{1}\to X_{H}$ in $\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}$}\\
\text{labeled by $\pi$ }\end{subarray}\right\}.

This concludes the proof of
Claim
3
.

With
Claim
2
and
Claim
3
in hand, we return to the analysis of the posterior
\Pr\left[\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]
. Letting
O\coloneqq t(H-1)
be the number of transitions we observe in
\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}
, we get that

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]\leq\frac{1}{2^{2H\cdot O}}\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{HT}.

(24)

We also have the lower bound that

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]\geq\frac{1}{2^{H-1}}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}}\Pr\left[\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]\geq\frac{2^{H-1}-T}{2^{H-1}}\cdot\frac{1}{2^{2H\cdot O}}\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{HT},

(25)

where the last inequality follows because for any filtration
\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}
we must have
\mathbbm{1}\left\{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{no path $X_{1}\to X_{H}$ in $\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}$}\\
\text{labeled by $\pi$ }\end{subarray}\right\}=1
for at least
2^{H-1}-T
such policies in
\Pi_{1:H-1}
.

Putting Eq. (
24
) and (
25
) together we get that

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\cdot\frac{\Pr\left[\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]}{\Pr\left[\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]}\leq\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H-2}}\right)^{2HT+1},

which in turn using Eq. (
23
) implies that

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\cdot\left\|\Pr\left[\pi^{\star}=\cdot\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]-\mathrm{Unif}(\Pi_{1:H-1})\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq 2\max_{\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}}\left[\frac{\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{F}\wedge\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\cdot\Pr\left[\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]}{\Pr\left[\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]}-1\right]_{+}\leq 2\left(\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H-2}}\right)^{2HT+1}-1\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2HT^{2}+T}{2^{H-3}}\exp\left(\frac{2HT^{2}+T}{2^{H-2}}\right)\leq\frac{HT^{2}}{2^{H-6}}.

We use the numerical inequalities
1+y\leq\exp(y)
and
\exp(y)-1\leq y\exp y
. This concludes the proof of
Lemma
10
.
∎

Lemma 11
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be any filtration of
HT
generative model samples as well as annotations
\phi(x)
for a subset of observations
x\in\mathcal{F}
. Let
\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}
be any policy. Fix any
h\geq 2
, and let
x_{\mathrm{new}}\in\mathcal{X}_{h}-\mathcal{F}
. Then

\displaystyle\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(x_{\mathrm{new}})=\mathsf{g}\mid\mathcal{F},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]-\left(1-\frac{1}{2^{H}}\right)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{T}{2^{H}},\quad\text{and}\quad\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(x_{\mathrm{new}})=\mathsf{b}\mid\mathcal{F},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]-\frac{1}{2^{H}}\right\rvert\leq\frac{T}{2^{H}}.

Proof of
Lemma
11
.

Let us denote
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
to be the completely annotated
\mathcal{F}
which includes all labels
\{\phi(X):X\in\mathcal{F}\}
. We will show that the conclusion of the lemma applies to every completion
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
, and since

\displaystyle\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(x_{\mathrm{new}})=\cdot\mid\mathcal{F},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]=\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(x_{\mathrm{new}})=\cdot\mid\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]\mid\mathcal{F},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right],

this will imply the result by Jensen’s inequality and convexity of
\lvert\cdot\rvert
.

We calculate the good label probability:

\displaystyle\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(x_{\mathrm{new}})=\mathsf{g}\mid\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]=\frac{2^{H}-\lvert\left\{X\in\mathcal{F}:\phi(X)=\mathsf{g}\right\}\rvert}{2^{2H}-\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert}.

For the lower bound we have

\displaystyle\frac{2^{H}-\lvert\left\{X\in\mathcal{F}:\phi(X)=\mathsf{g}\right\}\rvert}{2^{2H}-\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert}\geq\frac{2^{H}-T}{2^{2H}}=\frac{1}{2^{H}}\cdot\left(1-\frac{T}{{2^{H}}}\right).

For the upper bound we have

\displaystyle\frac{2^{H}-\lvert\left\{X\in\mathcal{F}:\phi(X)=\mathsf{g}\right\}\rvert}{2^{2H}-\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert}\leq\frac{2^{H}}{2^{2H}-T}=\frac{1}{2^{H}}\cdot\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{2H}}\right)^{-1}\leq\frac{1}{2^{H}}\cdot\left(1+\frac{T}{{2^{H}}}\right),

which holds as long as
T\leq 2^{H}
. Combining both upper and lower bounds proves the lemma for the good label. The calculation for
\phi(x_{\mathrm{new}})=\mathsf{b}
is similar, so we omit it. This concludes the proof of
Lemma
11
.
∎

D.4
Proof of
Theorem
3

The lower bound constructions for the proof of
Theorem
3
have a similar flavor to the lower bound construction in
Theorem
2
, but with a twist. In every layer, we include an additional distractor state
s^{\mathsf{d}}
which is not reachable from
d_{1}
but still sampled by
\mu
. The optimal policy at
s^{\mathsf{d}}_{H}
is the opposite of the optimal policy from the states on the good chain
s^{\mathsf{g}}_{H}
, and given only online access, the learner cannot distinguish between the good states and the distractor states.

Lower Bound Construction.

Again, the policy class
\Pi
is taken to be open loop policies:

\displaystyle\Pi\coloneqq\{\pi:\forall x\in\mathcal{X}_{h},\pi_{h}(x)\equiv a_{h},(a_{1},\cdots,a_{H})\in\mathcal{A}^{H}\}.

We define a family of Block MDPs
\mathcal{M}=\{M_{\pi^{\star},\phi}\}_{\pi^{\star}\in\Pi,\phi\in\Phi}
which are parameterized by an optimal policy
\pi^{\star}\in\Pi
and a decoding function
\phi\in\Phi
(to be described). An example is illustrated in
Figure
3
.

•

Latent MDP:
The latent state space
\mathcal{S}
is layered where each
\mathcal{S}_{h}\coloneqq\{s_{h}^{\mathsf{g}},s_{h}^{\mathsf{b}},s_{h}^{\mathsf{d}}\}
is comprised of a good, a bad, and a distractor state. We abbreviate the state as
\{\mathsf{g},\mathsf{b},\mathsf{d}\}
if the layer
h
is clear from context. The starting state is always
\mathsf{g}
. The action space
\mathcal{A}=\{0,1\}
. Let
\pi^{\star}\in\Pi
be any policy, which can be represented by a vector in
(\pi^{\star}_{1},\cdots,\pi^{\star}_{H})\in\{0,1\}^{H}
. The latent transitions/rewards of an MDP parameterized by
\pi^{\star}\in\Pi
are as follows for every
h\in[H]
:

\displaystyle P_{\mathsf{lat}}(\cdot\mid s,a)=\begin{cases}\delta_{s_{h+1}^{\mathsf{g}}}&\text{if}\penalty\ s=s_{h}^{\mathsf{g}},a=\pi^{\star}_{h}\\
\delta_{s_{h+1}^{\mathsf{d}}}&\text{if}\penalty\ s=s_{h}^{\mathsf{d}},a=\pi^{\star}_{h}\\
\delta_{s_{h+1}^{\mathsf{b}}}&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}\quad\text{and}\quad R_{\mathsf{lat}}(s,a)=\begin{cases}1&\text{if}\penalty\ s=s_{H}^{\mathsf{g}},a=\pi^{\star}_{H}\\
1&\text{if}\penalty\ s=s_{H}^{\mathsf{d}},a\neq\pi^{\star}_{H}\\
\mathrm{Ber}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)&\text{if}\penalty\ s=s_{H}^{\mathsf{b}}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

•

Rich Observations:
The observation state space
\mathcal{X}
is layered where each
\mathcal{X}_{h}\coloneqq\{x_{h}^{(1)},\cdots,x_{h}^{(m)}\}
with
m=2^{H+2}
. The decoding function class
\Phi
is the collection of all decoders which for every
h\geq 2
assigns
s_{h}^{\mathsf{g}},s_{h}^{\mathsf{d}}
to disjoint subsets of
\mathcal{X}_{h}
of size
2^{H}
and
s_{h}^{\mathsf{b}}
to the rest:

\displaystyle\Phi\coloneqq\Big\{\phi:\penalty\ \mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathcal{S}:\penalty\

\displaystyle\forall\penalty\ x_{1}\in\mathcal{X}_{1},\penalty\ \phi(x_{1})=\mathsf{g},

\displaystyle\forall\penalty\ h\geq 2,\penalty\ \left\lvert\left\{x_{h}\in\mathcal{X}_{h}:\phi(x_{h})=\mathsf{g}\right\}\right\rvert=2^{H}\text{ and }\left\lvert\left\{x_{h}\in\mathcal{X}_{h}:\phi(x_{h})=\mathsf{d}\right\}\right\rvert=2^{H}\Big\},

\displaystyle\hskip-80.00012pt\text{so that}\penalty\ \lvert\Phi\rvert=\left({2^{H+2}\choose 2^{H}}\cdot{2^{H+2}-2^{H}\choose 2^{H}}\right)^{H-1}=2^{2^{\widetilde{O}(H)}}.

In the MDP parameterized by
\phi\in\Phi
, the emission for every
s\in\mathcal{S}
is
\psi(s)=\mathrm{Unif}\left(\left\{x\in\mathcal{X}_{h}:\phi(x)=s\right\}\right)
.

•

Exploratory Distribution:
The exploratory distribution
\mu=\{\mu_{h}\}_{h\in[H]}
is set to be
\mu_{h}=\mathrm{Unif}(\mathcal{X}_{h})
.

We establish several facts about any
M_{\pi^{\star},\phi}\in\mathcal{M}
defined by the construction.

•

The distribution
\mu
has bounded concentrability:
C_{\mathsf{conc}}(\mu;\Pi,M)\leq 4
.

•

The policy class
\Pi
does not satisfy realizability, since the optimal policy at layer
H
requires one to take different actions depending on whether the latent state is
\mathsf{g}
or
\mathsf{d}
.

Sample Complexity Lower Bound.

We will use
Theorem
9
to prove our lower bound. First we need to instantiate the parameter space. We will let
\Theta\coloneqq\{(\pi^{\star},\phi):\penalty\ \pi^{\star}\in\Pi,\phi\in\Phi\}
so that
\mathcal{M}=\{M_{\theta}\}_{\theta\in\Theta}=\{M_{\pi^{\star},\phi}\}_{\pi^{\star}\in\Pi,\phi\in\Phi}
. We further denote the subsets

\displaystyle\Theta_{0}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{(\pi^{\star},\phi):\penalty\ \pi^{\star}\in\Pi\penalty\ \text{s.t.}\penalty\ \pi^{\star}_{H}=0,\phi\in\Phi\}

\displaystyle\Theta_{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{(\pi^{\star},\phi):\penalty\ \pi^{\star}\in\Pi\penalty\ \text{s.t.}\penalty\ \pi^{\star}_{H}=1,\phi\in\Phi\}

The observation space
\mathcal{Y}
is defined as the set of observations over
T
rounds as well as returned policy for an algorithm interacting with the MDP, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}\coloneqq(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{A}\times[0,1])^{HT}\times\Pi.

(As a convention, we can assume that each sample collected by
\mathsf{Alg}
in the MDP is of length
H
; if
\mathsf{Alg}
decides to rollout from
\mu_{h}
at an intermediate layer
h\geq 2
then we can simply append “dummy states” to the prefix of the trajectory, which does not change the analysis.)

For an observation
y\in\mathcal{Y}
we define the final returned policy as
y^{\pi}
. The loss function is given by

\displaystyle L((\pi^{\star},\phi),y)\coloneqq\mathbbm{1}\left\{\pi^{\star}\neq y^{\pi}\right\}.

Then we have for any
y\in\mathcal{Y}
,
(\pi^{\star}_{0},\phi_{0})\in\Theta_{0}
, and
(\pi^{\star}_{1},\phi_{1})\in\Theta_{1}
that

\displaystyle L((\pi^{\star}_{0},\phi_{0}),y)+L((\pi^{\star}_{1},\phi_{1}),y)\geq 1\coloneqq 2\Delta,

since the last bit of
y^{\pi}
can be either 0 or 1, thus only matching exactly one of
\pi^{\star}_{0}
and
\pi^{\star}_{1}
.

Now we are ready to apply
Theorem
9
. We get that for any
\mathsf{Alg}
, we must have

\displaystyle\sup_{(\pi^{\star},\phi)\in\Pi\times\Phi}\En_{Y\sim\Pr^{M_{\pi^{\star},\phi},\mathsf{Alg}}}\left[V^{\star}-V^{{\widehat{\pi}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sup_{(\pi^{\star},\phi)\in\Pi\times\Phi}\En_{Y\sim\Pr^{M_{\pi^{\star},\phi},\mathsf{Alg}}}\left[\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\pi^{\star}=Y^{\pi}\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sup_{(\pi^{\star},\phi)\in\Pi\times\Phi}\En_{Y\sim\Pr^{M_{\pi^{\star},\phi},\mathsf{Alg}}}\left[L((\pi^{\star},\phi),Y)\right]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{8}\cdot\max_{\nu_{0}\in\Delta(\Theta_{0}),\nu_{1}\in\Delta(\Theta_{1})}\left(1-D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}},\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{8}\cdot\left(1-D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{\mathrm{Unif}(\Theta_{0}),\mathsf{Alg}},\Pr^{\mathrm{Unif}(\Theta_{1}),\mathsf{Alg}}\right)\right).

It remains to compute an upper bound
D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{\mathrm{Unif}(\Theta_{0}),\mathsf{Alg}},\Pr^{\mathrm{Unif}(\Theta_{1}),\mathsf{Alg}}\right)
which holds for any
\mathsf{Alg}
. This is accomplished by the following lemma.

Lemma 12
.

For any deterministic
\mathsf{Alg}
that adaptively collects
T=2^{O(H)}
samples via
\mu
-reset access, we have

\displaystyle D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{\mathrm{Unif}(\Theta_{0}),\mathsf{Alg}},\Pr^{\mathrm{Unif}(\Theta_{1}),\mathsf{Alg}}\right)\leq\frac{T^{4}H}{2^{H-10}}.

Plugging in
Lemma
12
, we conclude that for any
\mathsf{Alg}
that collects
2^{cH}
samples for sufficiently small constant
c>0
must be
1/16
-suboptimal in expectation. This concludes the proof of
Theorem
3
.∎

D.5
Proof of
Lemma
12
(TV Distance Calculation for
Theorem
3
)

Since the proof is similar to that of
Lemma
5
we omit some intermediate calculations. In the rest of the proof we denote
\nu_{0}\coloneqq\mathrm{Unif}(\Theta_{0})
and
\nu_{1}\coloneqq\mathrm{Unif}(\Theta_{1})
. We have

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003ptD_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}},\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\right)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{h=1}^{H-1}\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h+1}\mid X_{t,h},A_{t,h},\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right],\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h+1}\mid X_{t,h},A_{t,h},\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right]\right)\right]}_{\text{transition TV distance}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\underbrace{\sum_{t=1}^{T}\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}\mid X_{t,H},A_{t,H},\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right],\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}\mid X_{t,H},A_{t,H},\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]\right)\right]}_{\text{reward TV distance}}.

We bound each term separately.

Transition TV Distance.

Using triangle inequality and
Lemma
13
we get that for any
t\in[T],h\in[H-1]
:

\displaystyle\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h+1}\mid X_{t,h},A_{t,h},\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right],\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h+1}\mid X_{t,h},A_{t,h},\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right]\right)\right]\leq\frac{t}{2^{H-3}}.

(26)

Reward TV Distance.

Using triangle inequality, the fact that rewards are in
\{0,1\}
, and
Lemma
14
we get

\displaystyle\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}\mid X_{t,H},A_{t,H},\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right],\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}\mid X_{t,H},A_{t,H},\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid X_{t,H},A_{t,H},\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\right\rvert\right]+\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid X_{t,H},A_{t,H},\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\right\rvert\right]

\displaystyle\leq t\cdot\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H-9}}.

(27)

Final Bound.

Thus, combining Eqs. (
26
) and (
27
) we can conclude that:

\displaystyle D_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}},\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\right)\leq\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H-3}}+\frac{T^{4}H}{2^{H-9}}\leq\frac{T^{4}H}{2^{H-10}}.

This concludes the proof of
Lemma
12
.∎

Lemma 13

(Transition TV Distance for the Construction in
Theorem
3
)
.

For any
t\in[T],h\in[H]
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right]-\mathrm{Unif}(\mathcal{X}_{h})\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{t}{2^{H-2}},

\displaystyle\left\|\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right]-\mathrm{Unif}(\mathcal{X}_{h})\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{t}{2^{H-2}}.

Proof of
Lemma
13
.

We prove the bound for
\nu_{0}
, as the proof for
\nu_{1}
is identical. If we sample
X_{t,h}
directly from the
\mu
-reset distribution, then the result immediately follows since the distribution of
X_{t,h}=\mathrm{Unif}(\mathcal{X}_{h})
. Otherwise, denote

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}^{\prime}=\sigma(\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1},\{\phi(X):X\in\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\},\{\mathbbm{1}\left\{A=\pi^{\star}(X)\right\}:(X,A)\in\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\})

to be the annotated sigma-field which also includes the latent state labels for all of the previous observions as well as whether the action taken followed
\pi^{\star}
or not. Let us denote
\ell=\phi(X_{t,h})\in\{\mathsf{g},\mathsf{b},\mathsf{d}\}
to be the latent state label of the next observation. Observe that the label
\ell
is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}^{\prime}
since the filtration
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}^{\prime}
includes
\phi(X_{t,h-1})
as well as
\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{t,h-1}=\pi^{\star}(X_{t,h-1})\right\}
. Furthermore denote
\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}
to denote the total number of observations that we have encountered already in layer
h
and
\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\ell}
to denote the observations we have encountered whose label is
\ell
.

Under the uniform distribution over decoders, the assignment of the remaining observations is equally likely. Therefore we can write the distribution of
X_{t,h}
as:

\displaystyle\text{if}\penalty\ \ell=\mathsf{g}:

\displaystyle\quad\Pr^{\mathrm{Unif}(\Theta_{0}),\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h}=x\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}^{\prime}\right]=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2^{H}}&\text{if}\penalty\ x\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\ell}\\
0&\text{if}\penalty\ x\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}-\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\ell}\\
\frac{1}{2^{H}}\cdot\frac{2^{H}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\ell}\rvert}{2^{H+2}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}\rvert}&\text{if}\penalty\ x\in\mathcal{X}_{h}-\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\text{if}\penalty\ \ell=\mathsf{b}:

\displaystyle\quad\Pr^{\mathrm{Unif}(\Theta_{0}),\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h}=x\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}^{\prime}\right]=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2^{H+1}}&\text{if}\penalty\ x\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\ell}\\
0&\text{if}\penalty\ x\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}-\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\ell}\\
\frac{1}{2^{H+1}}\cdot\frac{2^{H+1}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\ell}\rvert}{2^{H+2}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}\rvert}&\text{if}\penalty\ x\in\mathcal{X}_{h}-\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\text{if}\penalty\ \ell=\mathsf{d}:

\displaystyle\quad\Pr^{\mathrm{Unif}(\Theta_{0}),\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h}=x\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}^{\prime}\right]=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2^{H}}&\text{if}\penalty\ x\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\ell}\\
0&\text{if}\penalty\ x\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}-\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\ell}\\
\frac{1}{2^{H}}\cdot\frac{2^{H}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\ell}\rvert}{2^{H+2}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}\rvert}&\text{if}\penalty\ x\in\mathcal{X}_{h}-\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}\end{cases}

We elaborate on the calculation for the last probability in each case. Suppose
\ell=\mathsf{g}
. Then for any
x\in\mathcal{X}_{h}-\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}
which has not been observed yet we assign
\phi(x)=\ell
in

\displaystyle{2^{H+2}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}\rvert-1\choose 2^{H}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\ell}\rvert-1}\text{ ways out of }{2^{H+2}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}\rvert\choose 2^{H}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\ell}\rvert}\text{ assignments.}

\displaystyle\quad\Longrightarrow\phi(x)=\mathsf{g}\text{ with probability }\frac{2^{H}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\ell}\rvert}{2^{H+2}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}\rvert}.

For each assignment where
\phi(x)=\mathsf{g}
we will select it with probability
1/2^{H}
since the emission is uniform, giving us the final probability as claimed. A similar calculation can be done for the cases where
\ell=\mathsf{b},\mathsf{d}
.

Therefore we can calculate the final bound that

\displaystyle\left\|\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}^{\prime}\right]-\mathrm{Unif}(\mathcal{X}_{h})\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{h}}\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h}=x\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right]-\frac{1}{2^{H+2}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\begin{cases}\frac{\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\mathsf{g}}\rvert}{2^{H}}+\frac{\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\mathsf{b}}\rvert+\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\mathsf{d}}\rvert}{2^{H+2}}+\left\lvert\frac{2^{H}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\mathsf{g}}\rvert}{2^{H}}-\frac{2^{H+2}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}\rvert}{2^{H+2}}\right\rvert&\text{if}\penalty\ \ell=\mathsf{g},\\[5.0pt]
\frac{\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\mathsf{b}}\rvert}{2^{H+1}}+\frac{\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\mathsf{g}}\rvert+\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\mathsf{d}}\rvert}{2^{H+2}}+\left\lvert\frac{2^{H+1}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\mathsf{b}}\rvert}{2^{H+1}}-\frac{2^{H+2}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}\rvert}{2^{H+2}}\right\rvert&\text{if}\penalty\ \ell=\mathsf{b},\\[5.0pt]
\frac{\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\mathsf{d}}\rvert}{2^{H}}+\frac{\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\mathsf{b}}\rvert+\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\mathsf{g}}\rvert}{2^{H+2}}+\left\lvert\frac{2^{H}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}^{\mathsf{g}}\rvert}{2^{H}}-\frac{2^{H+2}-\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}\rvert}{2^{H+2}}\right\rvert&\text{if}\penalty\ \ell=\mathsf{d}.\end{cases}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4\cdot\lvert\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{obs}}\rvert}{2^{H}}\leq\frac{4t}{2^{H}}.

Since
\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right]=\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}[X_{t,h}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}^{\prime}]
, we have by convexity of
\ell_{1}
norm and Jensen’s inequality,

\displaystyle\left\|\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\right]-\mathrm{Unif}(\mathcal{X}_{h})\right\|_{1}\leq\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\left\|\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[X_{t,h}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}^{\prime}\right]-\mathrm{Unif}(\mathcal{X}_{h})\right\|_{1}\right]\leq\frac{4t}{2^{H}},

which concludes the proof of
Lemma
13
.
∎

Lemma 14

(Reward TV Distance for the Construction in
Theorem
3
)
.

For any
t\in[T]
we have

\displaystyle\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid X_{t,H},A_{t,H},\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\right\rvert\right]

\displaystyle\leq t\cdot\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H-8}},

\displaystyle\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid X_{t,H},A_{t,H},\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\right\rvert\right]

\displaystyle\leq t\cdot\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H-8}}.

Proof of
Lemma
14
.

Let us denote
\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\coloneqq\sigma(X_{t,H},A_{t,H},\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1})
.

We will prove the first inequality of
Lemma
14
; the second inequality is obtained using similar arguments.

Peeling Off Bad Event.

First, let us peel off the event that
\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}
has repeated observations: denoting
\mathcal{E}_{N}\coloneqq\{X_{t,h}\notin\mathcal{F}_{t,h-1}\penalty\ \forall t\in[T],h\in[H]\}
, we have

\displaystyle\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\right\rvert\right]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}[\mathcal{E}_{N}^{c}]+\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\right\rvert\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H}}+\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\right\rvert\right],

(28)

where the last inequality follows by an identical argument as
Lemma
9
. Therefore, it suffices to bound the expectation only for the
\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}
which have no repeated states.

Inductive Claim.

Now we define the event
\mathcal{E}_{R,t}
to be the event that among the first
t
episodes,
\mathsf{Alg}
never performs an online rollout (meaning it starts from layer 1) which follows
\pi^{\star}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{R,t}\coloneqq\left\{\forall t^{\prime}\leq t:A_{t,1:H-1}\neq\pi^{\star}\right\}.

A subtle point is that unlike the reward TV distance calculation for
Theorem
2
, the event
\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}
is
not measurable
with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}
(since there is still uncertainty as to what
\pi^{\star}
is). This causes some technical complications in the proof. To remedy this, we can consider working with an augmented filtration which appends a special token
\top
at the end of every online trajectory that
\mathsf{Alg}
takes if the sequence of actions
A_{t,1:H-1}
matches
\pi^{\star}
;
now

\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}
is measurable with respect to the augmented filtration (namely the event
\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}
holds if the augmented contains no special tokens
\top
). This augmentation does not affect the overall argument, and for the rest of the proof we assume that
\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}
has been augmented in this way.

Central to our proof is the following claim that
\mathcal{E}_{R,t}\cup\mathcal{E}_{N}
happens with high probability:

\displaystyle\text{for all}\penalty\ t\in[T]:\quad\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{R,t}^{c}\cup\mathcal{E}_{N}\right]\leq t\cdot\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H-7}}.

(29)

Now we will establish
Claim
29
using an inductive argument. The base case of
t=0
trivially holds. Now suppose that
Claim
29
holds at time
t-1
. Then

\displaystyle\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{R,t}^{c}\cup\mathcal{E}_{N}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}^{c}\cup\mathcal{E}_{N}\right]+\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[A_{t,1:H-1}=\pi^{\star}\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\leq(t-1)\cdot\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H-7}}+\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[A_{t,1:H-1}=\pi^{\star}\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\leq(t-1)\cdot\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H-7}}+\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}}\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{t,1:H-1}=\pi\right\}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\pi^{\star}=\pi\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\leq(t-1)\cdot\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H-7}}+\frac{1}{2^{H-1}}+\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H-6}}\leq t\cdot\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H-7}},

Here, the second-to-last inequality uses
Lemma
15
and the fact that
A_{t,H-1}
can only match a single policy
\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}
.

Casework on Reward TV Distance.

Armed with
Claim
29
, we now return to the proof of the reward TV distance calculation. We consider two cases. In the first case, the
t
-th trajectory is generated by an online rollout from
h=1
with the sequence of actions
A_{1:H}
. In the second case, the
t
-th trajectory is generated by first querying the
\mu
-reset model starting from
h_{\bot}\geq 2
, then rolling out with the sequence of actions
A_{h_{\bot}:H}
.

Case 1: Online Rollout from Layer 1.
First, we peel off the probability of
\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}
occuring:

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\right\rvert\right]

\displaystyle\leq\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}^{c}\cup\mathcal{E}_{N}\right]+\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\cdot\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\right\rvert\right]

\displaystyle\leq(t-1)\cdot\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H-7}}+\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\cdot\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\right\rvert\right].

(30)

Now we compute

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\phi(X_{t,H})=\mathsf{g}\wedge A_{t,H}=0\right\}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\phi(X_{t,H})=\mathsf{b}\right\}\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{=}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\left(\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[A_{t,1:H}=\pi^{\star}\circ 0\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]+\frac{1}{2}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[A_{t,1:H-1}\neq\pi^{\star}\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(ii)}{=}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}}\left(\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{t,1:H}=\pi\circ 0\right\}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{t,1:H-1}\neq\pi\right\}\right)\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\pi^{\star}=\pi\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}},\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(iii)}{\leq}\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H-6}}+\frac{\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}}{2^{H-1}}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}}\left(\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{t,1:H}=\pi\circ 0\right\}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{t,1:H-1}\neq\pi\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(iv)}{\leq}\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H-7}}+\frac{\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}}{2}.

(31)

For equality
(i)
we use the fact that if the
X_{t,H}
has a good label then we must have taken
\pi^{\star}
for the first
H-1
layers. For equality
(ii)
we use the fact that the indicators are measurable with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{t,H-1}
and
\{\pi^{\star}=\pi\}
. For
(iii)
we apply a change-of-measure argument using
Lemma
15
. For
(iv)
we use the fact that the sequence of actions
A_{t,1:H-1}
can match at exactly one of the policies in
\Pi_{1:H-1}
.

Note that the other side of the inequality can be shown analogously. Therefore by plugging in Eq. (
31
) into (
30
) we get the bound that

\displaystyle\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\right\rvert\right]\leq t\cdot\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H-7}}.

(32)

Case 2:
\mu
-Reset Rollout from Layer
h_{\bot}\geq 2
.
Let us analyze the second case. Using the construction details,

\displaystyle\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]

\displaystyle=\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\phi(X_{t,H})=\mathsf{g}\wedge A_{t,H}=0\right\}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\phi(X_{t,H})=\mathsf{b}\right\}+\mathbbm{1}\left\{\phi(X_{t,H})=\mathsf{d}\wedge A_{t,H}=1\right\}\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]

\displaystyle=\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\phi(X_{t,h_{\bot}})=\mathsf{g}\wedge A_{t,h_{\bot}:H}=\pi^{\star}\circ 0\right\}\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{2}\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{t,h_{\bot}:H-1}\neq\pi^{\star}\right\}+\mathbbm{1}\left\{\phi(X_{t,h_{\bot}})=\mathsf{b}\wedge A_{t,h_{\bot}:H-1}=\pi^{\star}\right\}\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\phi(X_{t,h_{\bot}})=\mathsf{d}\wedge A_{t,h_{\bot}:H}=\pi^{\star}\circ 1\right\}\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{h_{\bot}:H-1}}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\pi^{\star}=\pi\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]\bigg(\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{t,h_{\bot}:H}=\pi\circ 0\right\}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(X_{t,h_{\bot}})=\mathsf{g}\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{t,h_{\bot}:H-1}\neq\pi\right\}+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{t,h_{\bot}:H-1}=\pi\right\}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(X_{t,h_{\bot}})=\mathsf{b}\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{t,h_{\bot}:H}=\pi\circ 1\right\}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(X_{t,h_{\bot}})=\mathsf{d}\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]\bigg).

We apply
Lemma
17
separately to the terms inside the parentheses for every
\pi
. Then using a casework argument on the value of
A_{t,h_{\bot}:H}
and then averaging over the posterior of
\pi^{\star}
gives

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{N}\right\}\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\right\rvert\leq\frac{TH}{2^{H-5}}.

(33)

Putting It Together.

To conclude, the worst-case TV distance is the maximum of the two bounds we have shown in Eqs. (
32
) and (
33
), so therefore plugging into Eq. (
28
) we have

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[R_{t,H}=1\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\right\rvert\right]\leq\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H}}+\max\left\{t\cdot\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H-7}},\frac{TH}{2^{H-5}}\right\}\leq t\cdot\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H-8}}.

The proof of second inequality is obtained similarly, as one just needs to change the law to be under
\Pr^{\nu_{1},\mathsf{Alg}}
in the above argument. This concludes the proof of
Lemma
14
.
∎

Lemma 15

(Posterior of
\pi^{\star}
)
.

Fix any
t\in[T]
. Assume that
\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}
contains no repeated states. Then

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\right\}\cdot\left\|\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\pi^{\star}=\cdot\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]-\mathrm{Unif}(\Pi_{1:H-1})\right\|_{1}\leq\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H-6}}.

Proof.

In what follows, all of the probabilities
\Pr[\cdot]\coloneqq\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}[\cdot]
. Let
[x]_{+}\coloneqq\max\{x,0\}
. We can compute that

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\right\}\cdot\left\|\Pr\left[\pi^{\star}=\cdot\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]-\mathrm{Unif}(\Pi_{1:H-1})\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\right\}\cdot\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}}\left\lvert\Pr\left[\pi^{\star}=\pi\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]-\frac{1}{2^{H-1}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\right\}\cdot 2\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}}\left[\Pr\left[\pi^{\star}=\pi\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]-\frac{1}{2^{H-1}}\right]_{+}

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\right\}\cdot\frac{2}{2^{H-1}}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}}\left[\frac{\Pr\left[\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]}{\Pr\left[\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]}-1\right]_{+}

\displaystyle\leq 2\max_{\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}}\left[\frac{\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\right\}\cdot\Pr\left[\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]}{\Pr\left[\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]}-1\right]_{+}.

(34)

We now proceed by explicitly calculating the conditional distribution of
\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}
for every choice of optimal policy
\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}
. We will show that regardless of the choice
\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}
, the conditional distribution looks roughly like the uniform distribution over observations with a
\mathrm{Ber}(1/2)
reward at the end of every trajectory.

First, we will break up the distribution into trajectories:

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]=\left(\prod_{i<t}\Pr\left[\tau_{i}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]\right)\cdot\Pr\left[(X_{t,h_{\bot}:H},A_{t,h_{\bot}:H})\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right].

(35)

Claim 4
.

Fix any
i\in[t]
. If
\tau_{i}
is generated by sampling the
\mu
-reset distribution at some layer
h_{\bot}\geq 2
and then rolling out, we have for every
\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}
,

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\tau_{i}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]\in\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{2^{(H+2)\cdot(H-h_{\bot}+1)}}\cdot\left[\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1},\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1}\right]

We start by showing
Claim
4
. In our proof, we assume that
h_{\bot}=2
(the proof is easy to adapt to any
h_{\bot}\geq 2
with minor modification). Fix any index
i\in[t]
and let
\tau_{i}=(X_{2:H},A_{2:H},R)
be the
i
-th trajectory. Also fix any
\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}
. Then we can calculate

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\Pr\left[(X_{2:H},A_{2:H},R)\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\phi_{2}}\Pr\left[(X_{2:H},A_{2:H},R)\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\phi_{2}\right]\Pr\left[\phi_{2}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell\in\{\mathsf{g},\mathsf{b},\mathsf{d}\}}\sum_{\phi_{2}:\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\ell}\Pr\left[(X_{2:H},A_{2:H},R)\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\phi_{2}\right]\Pr\left[\phi_{2}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right].

We can separately analyze the sum for the different choices of the label of the initial state
X_{2}
. First, we do the case where
\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{b}
:

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\sum_{\phi_{2}:\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{b}}\Pr\left[(X_{2:H},A_{2:H},R)\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\phi_{2}\right]\Pr\left[\phi_{2}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\phi_{2}:\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{b}}\Pr\left[(X_{2},A_{2})\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\phi_{2}\right]\Pr\left[(X_{3:H},A_{3:H},R)\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\phi_{2},X_{2},A_{2}\right]\Pr\left[\phi_{2}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{=}\frac{1}{2^{H+2}}\sum_{\phi_{2}:\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{b}}\Pr\left[(X_{3:H},A_{3:H},R)\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\phi_{2},X_{2},A_{2}\right]\Pr\left[\phi_{2}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(ii)}{=}\frac{1}{2^{H+2}}\sum_{\phi_{2}:\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{b}}\Pr\left[(X_{3:H},A_{3:H},R)\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{b}\right]\Pr\left[\phi_{2}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\Pr\left[\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{b}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]}{2^{H+2}}\Pr\left[(X_{3:H},A_{3:H},R)\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{b}\right]

\displaystyle=\cdots

\displaystyle\overset{(iii)}{=}\frac{\Pr\left[\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{b}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]}{2^{H+2}}\times\frac{\Pr\left[\phi_{3}(X_{3})=\mathsf{b}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{b}\right]}{2^{H+1}}

\displaystyle\qquad\dots\times\frac{\Pr\left[\phi_{H}(X_{H})=\mathsf{b}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\left\{\phi_{h}(X_{h})=\mathsf{b},2\leq h\leq H-1\right\}\right]}{2^{H+1}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\Pr\left[R\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\left\{\phi_{h}(X_{h})=\mathsf{b},2\leq h\leq H\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\Pr\left[\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{b}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]}{2^{H+2}}\times\frac{\Pr\left[\phi_{3}(X_{3})=\mathsf{b}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{b}\right]}{2^{H+1}}

\displaystyle\qquad\dots\times\frac{\Pr\left[\phi_{H}(X_{H})=\mathsf{b}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\left\{\phi_{h}(X_{h})=\mathsf{b},2\leq h\leq H-1\right\}\right]}{2^{H+1}}\times\frac{1}{2}.

The equality
(i)
follows because the first state is chosen
\mathrm{Unif}(\mathcal{X}_{2})
and the action
A_{2}
is the one selected by
\mathsf{Alg}
via the policy
\pi^{(i)}
which is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{i-1}
. The equality
(ii)
follows because the distribution over the next state is only determined by
\mathcal{F}_{i-1}
(which includes some information about the decoder
\phi_{3}
) and the labeling
\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{b}
. Equality
(iii)
follows by applying chain rule over and over, noting that since
\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{b}
it must be the case that
\phi_{h}(X_{h})=\mathsf{b}
for all
h>2
, and therefore the probability of observing any given observation with a bad label is
1/2^{H+1}
.

Now we apply the posterior state label calculation of
Lemma
16
(using the fact that
\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}
contains no repeated states) to each term in the previous display to get that:

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\sum_{\phi_{2}:\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{b}}\Pr\left[(X_{2:H},A_{2:H},R)\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\phi_{2}\right]\Pr\left[\phi_{2}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]

\displaystyle\in\frac{1}{4}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2^{H+2}}\right)^{H-1}\cdot\left[\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1},\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1}\right].

(36)

Next, we consider other terms in the sum. To bound the quantity

\displaystyle\sum_{\phi_{2}:\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{g}}\Pr\left[(X_{2:H},A_{2:H},R)\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\phi_{2}\right]\Pr\left[\phi_{2}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right],

a bit more care is required. In this case, we start off in the good latent state, and depending on whether the sequence of actions
A_{2:H}
is equal to
\pi^{\star}
we transit to the bad latent state. Let us denote
\overline{h}\geq 2
denote the first layer at which
a_{\overline{h}}
deviates from
\pi_{\overline{h}}
. Then using similar reasoning we have

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\sum_{\phi_{2}:\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{g}}\Pr\left[(X_{2:H},A_{2:H},R)\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\phi_{2}\right]\Pr\left[\phi_{2}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\Pr\left[\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{g}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]}{2^{H+2}}\times\cdots\times\frac{\Pr\left[\phi_{\overline{h}}(X_{\overline{h}})=\mathsf{g}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\left\{\phi_{h}(X_{h})=\mathsf{g},\penalty\ \forall h<\overline{h}\right\}\right]}{2^{H}}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\frac{\Pr\left[\phi_{\overline{h}+1}(X_{\overline{h}+1})=\mathsf{b}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\left\{\phi_{h}(X_{h})=\mathsf{g},\penalty\ \forall h\leq\overline{h}\right\}\right]}{2^{H+1}}

\displaystyle\qquad\cdots\times\frac{\Pr\left[\phi_{H}(X_{H})=\mathsf{b}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\left\{\phi_{h}(X_{h})=\mathsf{g},\penalty\ \forall h\leq\overline{h}\right\},\left\{\phi_{h}(X_{h})=\mathsf{b},\penalty\ \forall\overline{h}<h<H\right\}\right]}{2^{H+1}}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\Pr\left[R\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\left\{\phi_{h}(X_{h})=\mathsf{g},\penalty\ \forall h\leq\overline{h}\right\},\left\{\phi_{h}(X_{h})=\mathsf{b},\penalty\ \forall\overline{h}<h<H\right\}\right].

Note that the conditional reward distribution given the latent state labels is

\displaystyle\Pr\left[R=1\mid\cdots\right]=\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{2:H}=\pi\circ 0\right\}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{2:H-1}\neq\pi\right\}.

Again by applying the posterior calculation of
Lemma
16
we get

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\sum_{\phi_{2}:\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{g}}\Pr\left[(X_{2:H},A_{2:H},R)\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\phi_{2}\right]\Pr\left[\phi_{2}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]

\displaystyle\in\begin{cases}\left(\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{2:H}=\pi\circ 0\right\}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{2:H-1}\neq\pi\right\}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{1}{2^{H+2}}\right)^{H-1}\cdot\left[\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1},\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1}\right]&\text{if}\penalty\ R=1\\[5.0pt]
\left(\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{2:H}=\pi\circ 1\right\}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{2:H-1}\neq\pi\right\}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{1}{2^{H+2}}\right)^{H-1}\cdot\left[\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1},\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1}\right]&\text{if}\penalty\ R=0\end{cases}

(37)

The last term in the sum, with
\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{d}
is similar, so we get that

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\sum_{\phi_{2}:\phi_{2}(X_{2})=\mathsf{d}}\Pr\left[(X_{2:H},A_{2:H},R)\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1},\phi_{2}\right]\Pr\left[\phi_{2}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]

\displaystyle\in\begin{cases}\left(\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{2:H}=\pi\circ 1\right\}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{2:H-1}\neq\pi\right\}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{1}{2^{H+2}}\right)^{H-1}\cdot\left[\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1},\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1}\right]&\text{if}\penalty\ R=1\\[5.0pt]
\left(\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{2:H}=\pi\circ 0\right\}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{2:H-1}\neq\pi\right\}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{1}{2^{H+2}}\right)^{H-1}\cdot\left[\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1},\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1}\right]&\text{if}\penalty\ R=0\end{cases}

(38)

(Note that the first indicators have been swapped in the previous display compared to Eq. (
37
).)

Summing Eqs. (
36
), (
37
), and (
38
), and applying casework on the different choices of
A_{2:H}
we get that

\displaystyle\Pr\left[(X_{2:H},A_{2:H},R)\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]\in\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2^{H+2}}\right)^{H-1}\cdot\left[\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1},\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1}\right],

thus concluding the proof of
Claim
4
.

Claim 5
.

If
\tau_{i}
is generated by an online rollout, we have for every
\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}
,

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\right\}\Pr\left[\tau_{i}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]\in\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\right\}\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{2^{(H+2)\cdot H}}\cdot\left[\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H},\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H}\right].

Now we prove
Claim
5
. Most of the hard work has already been done in the proof of
Claim
4
. Note that by construction
\phi_{1}(X_{1})=\mathsf{g}
. Using a similar calculation we have

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\Pr\left[(X_{1:H},A_{1:H},r)\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]

\displaystyle\in\begin{cases}\left(\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{1:H}=\pi\circ 0\right\}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{1:H-1}\neq\pi\right\}\right)\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2^{H+2}}\right)^{H}\cdot\left[\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H},\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H}\right]&\text{if}\penalty\ R=1\\[5.0pt]
\left(\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{1:H}=\pi\circ 1\right\}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{1}\left\{A_{1:H-1}\neq\pi\right\}\right)\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2^{H+2}}\right)^{H}\cdot\left[\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H},\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H}\right]&\text{if}\penalty\ R=0\end{cases}

However, observe that under the event
\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}
we know that
A_{1:H-1}\neq\pi^{\star}
, so the first indicator cannot be
=1
in either case; so multiplying both sides of the previous display by
\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\right\}
gives us the result of
Claim
5
.

To tidy up, we also state the calculation on the last trajectory, which does not include the prefactor of
\frac{1}{2}
because there are no observed rewards at the end:

Claim 6
.

\displaystyle\Pr\left[(X_{t,h_{\bot}:H},A_{t,h_{\bot}:H})\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi,\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]\in\frac{1}{2^{(H+2)\cdot(H-h_{\bot}+1)}}\left[\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H},\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H}\right].

Now with
Claim
4
,
5
, and
6
in hand, we can finally return to computing a bound on Eq. (
34
). Letting
O
denote the total number of observations in
\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}
(which can be at most
TH
), we have for any
\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}
,

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\right\}\Pr\left[\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]

\displaystyle\in\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\right\}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{t-1}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2^{H+2}}\right)^{O}\cdot\left[\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{TH},\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{TH}\right]\eqqcolon\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\right\}\cdot[\underline{B},\overline{B}].

Moreover, for any
\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}
we have

\displaystyle\Pr\left[\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]=\frac{1}{2^{H-1}}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}}\Pr\left[\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]\geq\frac{2^{H-1}-T}{2^{H-1}}\cdot\underline{B}.

The last inequality follows because there are at most
T
different action sequences which have been executed by online trajectories in
\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}
, so therefore for all but at most
T
policies we have
\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\right\}\Pr\left[\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]=\Pr\left[\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]
. Thus we arrive at the bound

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\right\}\left\|\Pr\left[\pi^{\star}=\cdot\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]-\mathrm{Unif}(\Pi_{1:H-1})\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq 2\max_{\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}}\left[\frac{\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathcal{E}_{R,t-1}\right\}\Pr\left[\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\mid\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]}{\Pr\left[\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}\right]}-1\right]_{+}\leq 2\left[\frac{\overline{B}}{\left(1-T/{2^{H-1}}\right)\cdot\underline{B}}-1\right]_{+}

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot\left(\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H-2}}\right)^{2TH+1}-1\right)\leq 2\cdot\frac{2T^{2}H+T}{2^{H-2}}\exp\left(\frac{2T^{2}H+T}{2^{H-2}}\right)\leq\frac{T^{2}H}{2^{H-6}}.

The second to last inequality uses the fact that
1+y\leq e^{y}
and
e^{y}-1\leq ye^{y}
, and the last inequality uses the fact that
T=2^{O(H)}
. This concludes the proof of
Lemma
15
.
∎

Lemma 16

(Posterior of New State Label)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be any filtration of
T
trajectories as well as annotations
\phi(x)
for a subset of observations
x\in\mathcal{F}
. Let
\pi\in\Pi_{1:H-1}
be any policy. Fix any
h\geq 2
, and let
x_{\mathrm{new}}\in\mathcal{X}_{h}-\mathcal{F}
. Then

\displaystyle\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(x_{\mathrm{new}})=\mathsf{g}\mid\mathcal{F},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]-\frac{1}{4}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{T}{2^{H}},

\displaystyle\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(x_{\mathrm{new}})=\mathsf{d}\mid\mathcal{F},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]-\frac{1}{4}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{T}{2^{H}},

\displaystyle\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(x_{\mathrm{new}})=\mathsf{b}\mid\mathcal{F},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]-\frac{1}{2}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{T}{2^{H}}.

Proof.

Let us denote
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
to be the completely annotated
\mathcal{F}
which includes all labels
\{\phi(X):X\in\mathcal{F}\}
. We will show that the conclusion of the lemma applies to every completion
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
, and since

\displaystyle\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(x_{\mathrm{new}})=\cdot\mid\mathcal{F},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]=\En^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(x_{\mathrm{new}})=\cdot\mid\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]\mid\mathcal{F},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right],

this will imply the result by Jensen’s inequality and convexity of
\lvert\cdot\rvert
.

We calculate the good label probability:

\displaystyle\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(x_{\mathrm{new}})=\mathsf{g}\mid\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]=\frac{2^{H}-\lvert\left\{X\in\mathcal{F}:\phi(X)=\mathsf{g}\right\}\rvert}{2^{H+2}-\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert}.

For the lower bound we have

\displaystyle\frac{2^{H}-\lvert\left\{X\in\mathcal{F}:\phi(X)=\mathsf{g}\right\}\rvert}{2^{H+2}-\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert}\geq\frac{2^{H}-T}{2^{H+2}}=\frac{1}{4}\cdot\left(1-\frac{T}{{2^{H}}}\right).

For the upper bound we have

\displaystyle\frac{2^{H}-\lvert\left\{x\in\mathcal{F}:\phi(x)=\mathsf{g}\right\}\rvert}{2^{H+2}-\lvert\mathcal{F}\rvert}\leq\frac{2^{H}}{2^{H+2}-T}=\frac{1}{4}\cdot\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{H+2}}\right)^{-1}\leq\frac{1}{4}\cdot\left(1+\frac{T}{{2^{H}}}\right),

which holds as long as
T\leq 2^{H}
. Combining both upper and lower bounds proves the lemma for the good label. The rest of the calculations are similar, so we omit them. This concludes the proof of
Lemma
16
.
∎

Lemma 17

(Posterior of State Label with Rollout)
.

Fix any
t\in[T]
. Suppose that episode
t
is sampled using the
\mu
-reset at layer
h_{\bot}\geq 2
, and that
\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}}
contains no repeated states. Then for any
\pi\in\Pi_{h_{\bot}:H-1}
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(X_{t,h_{\bot}})=\mathsf{g}\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]-\frac{1}{4}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{TH}{2^{H-3}},

\displaystyle\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(X_{t,h_{\bot}})=\mathsf{d}\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]-\frac{1}{4}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{TH}{2^{H-3}},

\displaystyle\left\lvert\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(X_{t,h_{\bot}})=\mathsf{b}\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]-\frac{1}{2}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{TH}{2^{H-3}}.

Proof.

We will prove the result with
h_{\bot}=2
, and it is easy to adapt it to the general case (in fact the setting where
h_{\bot}>2
only results in tighter bounds). Using repeated application of chain rule and
Lemma
16
we get

\displaystyle\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(X_{t,2})=\mathsf{g}\wedge(X_{t,2:H},A_{t,2:H})\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]

\displaystyle\in\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{1}{2^{H+2}}\right)^{H-1}\cdot\left[\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1},\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1}\right]

\displaystyle\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(X_{t,2})=\mathsf{b}\wedge(X_{t,2:H},A_{t,2:H})\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]

\displaystyle\in\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1}{2^{H+2}}\right)^{H-1}\cdot\left[\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1},\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1}\right]

\displaystyle\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(X_{t,2})=\mathsf{d}\wedge(X_{t,2:H},A_{t,2:H})\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]

\displaystyle\in\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{1}{2^{H+2}}\right)^{H-1}\cdot\left[\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1},\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{H-1}\right].

Let’s prove the first inequality in the lemma statement. By Bayes Rule we have

\displaystyle\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(X_{t,2})=\mathsf{g}\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(X_{t,2})=\mathsf{g}\wedge(X_{t,2:H},A_{t,2:H})\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]}{\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[(X_{t,2:H},A_{t,2:H})\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(X_{t,2})=\mathsf{g}\wedge(X_{t,2:H},A_{t,2:H})\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]}{\sum_{\ell\in\{\mathsf{g},\mathsf{b},\mathsf{d}\}}\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(X_{t,2})=\ell\wedge(X_{t,2:H},A_{t,2:H})\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]}.

From here it is easy to compute the upper bound

\displaystyle\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(X_{t,2})=\mathsf{g}\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]\leq\frac{1}{4}\cdot\left(1+\frac{T}{2^{H-1}}\right)^{2H}\leq\frac{1}{4}+\frac{TH}{2^{H-3}}.

as well as the lower bound

\displaystyle\Pr^{\nu_{0},\mathsf{Alg}}\left[\phi(X_{t,2})=\mathsf{g}\mid\underline{\mathcal{F}_{t,H}},\pi^{\star}=\pi\right]\geq\frac{1}{4}\cdot\left(1-\frac{T}{2^{H}}\right)^{2H}\geq\frac{1}{4}-\frac{TH}{2^{H-3}}.

The other two inequalities are similarly shown, and this concludes the proof of
Lemma
17
.
∎

Appendix E
Proof for the Warmup Algorithm
PLHR.D

In this section, we prove the following sample complexity guarantee for
PLHR.D
:

Theorem
5
.

Let
\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
be given and suppose that
Assumption
1
holds. Then with probability at least
1-\delta
,
PLHR.D
(
Algorithm
1
) finds an
\varepsilon
-optimal policy using

\displaystyle\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{S^{5}A^{2}H^{5}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\cdot\log\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\quad\text{samples.}

E.1
Proof of
Theorem
5

Our high-level strategy is to apply the inductive argument outlined in
Section
5.2
to control the growth of the Bellman error for all
(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}_{h}\times\mathcal{A}
as we construct
\widehat{M}_{\mathsf{lat}}
from layer
H
backwards. Recall our Bellman error decomposition:

\displaystyle\left\lvert Q^{\pi}(s,a)-\widehat{Q}^{\pi}(s,a)\right\rvert\leq\underbrace{\left\lvert R_{\mathsf{lat}}-\widehat{R}_{\mathsf{lat}}\right\rvert}_{\text{reward error}}+\underbrace{\left\lvert\widehat{V}^{\pi}(P_{\mathsf{lat}})-\widehat{V}^{\pi}(\widehat{P}_{\mathsf{lat}})\right\rvert}_{\text{transition error}}+\underbrace{\left\lvert V^{\pi}(P_{\mathsf{lat}})-\widehat{V}^{\pi}(P_{\mathsf{lat}})\right\rvert}_{\text{error at next layer}}.

(
3
)

To control the transition error of Eq. (
3
), we introduce a notion of test policy validity and give a lemma which shows that if
Decoder.D
is equipped with valid test policies, the transition estimation error can be bounded.

Definition 12

(Test Policy Validity, Deterministic Version)
.

Let
\eta>0
be a parameter. At layer
h\in[H]
, we say a collection of partial policies
\Pi^{\mathsf{test}}_{h}=\{\pi_{s,s^{\prime}}\in\Pi_{h:H}:s,s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{h}\}
is an
\eta
-
valid test policy set
for the estimated latent MDP
\widehat{M}_{\mathsf{lat}}
if for every
s,s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{h}
:

•

(Maximally distinguishing):
\pi_{s,s^{\prime}}=\argmax_{\pi\in\Pi_{h:H}}\lvert\widehat{V}^{\pi}(s)-\widehat{V}^{\pi}(s^{\prime})\rvert
.

•

(Accurate):
\lvert V^{\pi_{s,s^{\prime}}}(s)-\widehat{V}^{\pi_{s,s^{\prime}}}(s)\rvert\leq\eta
and
\lvert V^{\pi_{s,s^{\prime}}}(s^{\prime})-\widehat{V}^{\pi_{s,s^{\prime}}}(s^{\prime})\rvert\leq\eta
.

Lemma 18

(Decoding)
.

Fix any layer
h\in[H-1]
. Suppose that
Decoder.D
(
Algorithm
2
) is equipped with a
\epsilon_{\mathsf{tol}}
-valid test policy
\Pi^{\mathsf{test}}_{h+1}
. Fix any tuple
(s_{h},a_{h})
and assume that
P_{\mathsf{lat}}(s_{h},a_{h})\in\mathcal{P}(s_{h},a_{h})
. With high probability,
Decoder.D
returns an updated
\mathcal{P}
such that:

1.

P_{\mathsf{lat}}(s_{h},a_{h})\in\mathcal{P}
;

2.

For every
\bar{s}\in\mathcal{P}
we have
\max_{\pi\in\Pi}\penalty\ \lvert\widehat{V}^{\pi}(P_{\mathsf{lat}}(s_{h},a_{h}))-\widehat{V}^{\pi}(\bar{s})\rvert\leq 7\epsilon_{\mathsf{tol}}/2
.

The proof of
Lemma
18
is deferred to
Appendix
E.2
.

In light of
Lemma
18
, as long as we have valid test policy sets
\{\Pi^{\mathsf{test}}_{h}\}_{h\in[H]}
,
Lemma
18
provides control on the transition estimation error, and we can iteratively apply Eq. (
3
) to get the final bound on estimation error at layer 1.

Computing Test Policies via
Refit.D
.

Now we will analyze
Refit.D
. By standard concentration arguments, if
line
8
is triggered, the test policies must be
\epsilon_{\mathsf{tol}}
-accurate; furthermore, they are maximally distinguishing by construction. Unfortunately, since we require the test policies to satisfy a higher level of accuracy
\epsilon_{\mathsf{tol}}
, due to estimation errors in
\widehat{M}_{\mathsf{lat}}
, it may not be possible to find any valid test policies. To address this, we observe that inaccurate test policies act as a “certificate” and allow us to search for some transition
\widehat{P}_{\mathsf{lat}}\neq P_{\mathsf{lat}}
.

Lemma 19

(Refitting)
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
be given. Suppose that at layer
h\in[H]
,
Refit.D
(
Algorithm
3
) is supplied confidence sets
\mathcal{P}
such that for all
(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}_{h:H}\times\mathcal{A}
we have
P_{\mathsf{lat}}(s,a)\in\mathcal{P}(s,a)
. If
Refit.D
terminates at
line
17
, then with high probability:

1.

At least one
\widehat{P}_{\mathsf{lat}}
was removed from its confidence set
\mathcal{P}
.

2.

No ground truth transitions
P_{\mathsf{lat}}
are removed from their confidence set
\mathcal{P}
.

The proof of
Lemma
19
is deferred to
Appendix
E.2
.

Our analysis will track the invariant that the confidence sets
\mathcal{P}
always contain the ground truth transition
P_{\mathsf{lat}}
. Therefore,
Lemma
19
allows us to use the size of the confidence sets as a potential function: if
Refit.D
fails to compute valid test policies at some layer
h
, we must delete some incorrect transition
\widehat{P}_{\mathsf{lat}}
from its set
\mathcal{P}
; this process cannot continue indefinitely, since we can delete at most
S(S-1)A
states.

Proof by Induction.

With
Lemma
18
and
Lemma
19
in hand, we can show the final bound in
Theorem
5
. For technical convenience, we will show that the policy returned by
PLHR.D
is
O(\varepsilon)
suboptimal; rescaling the parameter
\varepsilon
does not change the final sample complexity apart from constant factors. Also, we omit the standard arguments (via concentration and union bound) which show that the conclusions of
Lemma
18
and
Lemma
19
hold with probability at least
1-\delta
over the randomness of sampling episodes from the MDP.

Take
\Gamma_{h}\coloneqq C(H-h+1)/H\cdot\varepsilon
for some suitably large constant
C>0
. We will inductively show that these properties hold for all layers
h\in[H]
:

1.

Policy Evaluation Accuracy.
For all pairs
(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}_{h}\times\mathcal{A}
and
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathsf{open}}
:
\lvert Q^{\pi}(s,a)-\widehat{Q}^{\pi}(s,a)\rvert\leq\Gamma_{h}
.

2.

Confidence Set Validity.
For all pairs
(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}_{h}\times\mathcal{A}
, we have
P_{\mathsf{lat}}(s,a)\in\mathcal{P}(s,a)
.

3.

Test Policy Validity.

\Pi^{\mathsf{test}}_{h}
are
\epsilon_{\mathsf{tol}}
-valid for
\widehat{M}_{\mathsf{lat}}
at layer
h
.

To analyze
PLHR.D
, we will show that these properties always hold throughout at the end of every while loop for all layers
h>\ell_{\mathsf{next}}
.

Base Case.
We analyze the first loop with
\ell=H
. Note that (A) holds by concentration of the reward estimates, and (B) trivially holds because there are no transitions to be constructed at layer
H
. Now we investigate what happens when
Refit.D
is called. The computed test policies take the form
\pi_{s,s^{\prime}}\equiv a
for some
a\in\mathcal{A}
; again by concentration of the reward estimates,
line
8
of
Refit.D
is triggered. Therefore (A)–(C) hold after refitting, and we jump to
\ell_{\mathsf{next}}=H-1
.

Inductive Step.
Suppose the current layer index is
\ell
, and that properties (A)–(C) hold for all
h>\ell
. By
Lemma
18
, the updated transition confidence sets returned by
Decoder.D
at layer
\ell
will satisfy (B). Furthermore, at the end of
line
10
, the error decomposition (
3
) implies that for every
(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}_{\ell}\times\mathcal{A}
:

\displaystyle\max_{\pi\in\Pi}\penalty\ \left\lvert Q^{\pi}(s,a)-\widehat{Q}^{\pi}(s,a)\right\rvert\leq\Gamma_{\ell+1}+\frac{\varepsilon}{H^{2}}+\frac{7\epsilon_{\mathsf{tol}}}{2}\leq\Gamma_{\ell},\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\text{Property (A) holds at layer $\ell$.}

Now we do casework on the outcome of
Refit.D
.

•

Case 1: Return in
line
8
.
By construction, property (C) is satisfied for layer
\ell
. In this case, since
Algorithm
3
made no updates to
\widehat{M}_{\mathsf{lat}}
or
\mathcal{P}
, properties (A) and (B) continue to hold at layer
\ell
onwards.

•

Case 2: Return in
line
17
.
By
Lemma
19
, any updates to
\widehat{M}_{\mathsf{lat}}
maintain property (B). Let
\ell_{\mathsf{next}}
denote the layer at which we jump to. By definition of
\ell_{\mathsf{next}}
, we made no updates to
\widehat{M}_{\mathsf{lat}}
at layers
\ell_{\mathsf{next}}+1
onwards, and therefore the previously computed test policies
\Pi^{\mathsf{test}}_{\ell_{\mathsf{next}}+1:H}
must still be valid, so therefore properties (A) and (C) continue to hold at layer
\ell_{\mathsf{next}}
onwards.

Continuing the induction, once
\ell\leftarrow 0
is reached in
PLHR.D
(which we know will eventually happen because Case 2 can only occur for
S^{2}A
times), the estimated latent MDP
\widehat{M}_{\mathsf{lat}}
must satisfy the bound

\displaystyle\max_{\pi\in\Pi}\penalty\ \left\lvert V^{\pi}(s_{1})-\widehat{V}^{\pi}(s_{1})\right\rvert\leq\Gamma_{1}=O(\varepsilon).

Sample Complexity Bound.

We now compute the final sample complexity required by
PLHR.D
:

•

Estimating rewards in the main algorithm uses
\widetilde{O}(H^{4}SA/\varepsilon^{2})
samples.

•

Decoder.D
is called at most
SA\times S^{2}A
times, since we (re-)decode every transition
(s,a)
at most
S^{2}A
times. Every call to
Decoder.D
uses
\widetilde{O}(S^{2}/\epsilon_{\mathsf{tol}}^{2})=\widetilde{O}(S^{2}H^{2}/\varepsilon^{2})
samples since we take
\epsilon_{\mathsf{tol}}=2^{5}\cdot\varepsilon/H
in
Lemma
19
. Therefore the total number of samples used by
Decoder.D
is at most
\widetilde{O}(S^{5}A^{2}H^{2}/\varepsilon^{2})
.

•

Refit.D
is called at most
S^{2}AH
times, since associated to every layer revisiting is an additional
H
calls in the main while loop. In every call to
Refit.D
, we use
\widetilde{O}(S^{2}H^{2}/\varepsilon^{2})
calls to compute and verify the test policy set in
line
5
. In addition, every time
line
10
is triggered corresponds to at least one deletion in
line
16
, so the number of additional samples used by
line
13
(across all calls to
Refit.D
) can be bounded by
\widetilde{O}(S^{2}AH^{3}/\epsilon_{\mathsf{tol}}^{2})=\widetilde{O}(S^{2}AH^{5}/\varepsilon^{2})
.

Thus the final sample complexity is at most
\widetilde{O}(S^{5}A^{2}H^{5}/\varepsilon^{2})
samples. ∎

E.2
Proof of Induction Lemmas

Proof of
Lemma
18
.

First we prove implication (1). Let us denote
s^{\star}=P_{\mathsf{lat}}(s_{h},a_{h})
. If
s^{\star}\notin\mathcal{P}
(the returned set), then there exists some
s^{\prime}
for which

\displaystyle\left\lvert V_{\mathsf{mc}}(x_{h+1}\mid\pi_{s^{\star},s^{\prime}})-\widehat{V}^{\pi_{s^{\star},s^{\prime}}}(s^{\star})\right\rvert\geq 2\epsilon_{\mathsf{tol}}.

However, by assumption of test policy accuracy we know that

\displaystyle\left\lvert V^{\pi_{s^{\star},s^{\prime}}}(s^{\star})-\widehat{V}^{\pi_{s^{\star},s^{\prime}}}(s^{\star})\right\rvert\leq\epsilon_{\mathsf{tol}}.

Since the quantity
V_{\mathsf{mc}}(x_{h+1}\mid\pi_{s^{\star},s^{\prime}})
is an unbiased estimate of
V^{\pi_{s^{\star},s^{\prime}}}(s^{\star})
which is estimated to accuracy
\epsilon_{\mathsf{tol}}/2
we have a contradiction, so
s^{\star}\in\mathcal{P}
.

Now we prove implication (2). If
\bar{s}\in\mathcal{P}
, then we must have

\displaystyle\left\lvert V_{\mathsf{mc}}(x_{h+1}\mid\pi_{s^{\star},\bar{s}})-\widehat{V}^{\pi_{s^{\star},\bar{s}}}(\bar{s})\right\rvert=\left\lvert V_{\mathsf{mc}}(s^{\star}\mid\pi_{s^{\star},\bar{s}})-\widehat{V}^{\pi_{s^{\star},\bar{s}}}(\bar{s})\right\rvert\leq 2\epsilon_{\mathsf{tol}}.

Since we estimated
V_{\mathsf{mc}}(s^{\star}\mid\pi_{s^{\star},\bar{s}})
up to
\epsilon_{\mathsf{tol}}/2
accuracy we know that

\displaystyle\left\lvert V^{\pi_{s^{\star},\bar{s}}}(s^{\star})-\widehat{V}^{\pi_{s^{\star},\bar{s}}}(\bar{s})\right\rvert\leq 5\epsilon_{\mathsf{tol}}/2,\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\left\lvert\widehat{V}^{\pi_{s^{\star},\bar{s}}}(s^{\star})-\widehat{V}^{\pi_{s^{\star},\bar{s}}}(\bar{s})\right\rvert\leq 7\epsilon_{\mathsf{tol}}/2,

where the implication follows by the accuracy of
\Pi^{\mathsf{test}}_{h+1}
.
By the maximal distinguishing property of
\Pi^{\mathsf{test}}_{h+1}
, observe that the LHS of the above implication is equal to
\max_{\pi\in\Pi}\penalty\ \lvert\widehat{V}^{\pi}(s^{\star})-\widehat{V}^{\pi}(\bar{s})\rvert
. This proves the second implication, and concludes the proof of
Lemma
18
.
∎

Proof of
Lemma
19
.

We show the first implication. Let
(s_{h},\pi)
be any policy which satisfies
\lvert V_{\mathsf{mc}}(s_{h}\mid\pi)-\widehat{V}^{\pi}(s_{h})\rvert\geq\epsilon_{\mathsf{tol}}-\varepsilon/H
. Since we estimated
V^{\pi}(s_{h})
up to
\varepsilon/H
error, we have
\lvert V^{\pi}(s_{h})-\widehat{V}^{\pi}(s_{h})\rvert\geq\epsilon_{\mathsf{tol}}-2\varepsilon/H
. Let
\bar{s}_{h}=s_{h},\bar{s}_{h+1},\cdots,\bar{s}_{H}
be the sequence of states which are obtained by running
\pi
on
\widehat{M}_{\mathsf{lat}}
starting at
s_{h}
.

For sake of contradiction suppose that

\displaystyle\left\lvert V_{\mathsf{mc}}(\bar{s}\mid\pi)-\widehat{R}(\bar{s},\pi)-V_{\mathsf{mc}}(\widehat{P}_{\mathsf{lat}}(\bar{s},\pi)\mid\pi)\right\rvert\leq\frac{4\varepsilon}{H^{2}},\quad\text{for all}\penalty\ \bar{s}\in\{\bar{s}_{h},\cdots,\bar{s}_{H}\}.

Since we estimated every
V_{\mathsf{mc}}(\cdot\mid\pi)
up to accuracy
\varepsilon/H^{2}
we see that

\displaystyle\left\lvert V^{\pi}(\bar{s})-\widehat{R}(\bar{s},\pi)-V^{\pi}(\widehat{P}_{\mathsf{lat}}(\bar{s},\pi))\right\rvert\leq\frac{6\varepsilon}{H^{2}},\quad\text{for all}\penalty\ \bar{s}\in\{\bar{s}_{h},\cdots,\bar{s}_{H}\}.

By Performance Difference Lemma and applying the previous display recursively,

\displaystyle\left\lvert V^{\pi}(s_{h})-\widehat{V}^{\pi}(s_{h})\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert V^{\pi}(\bar{s}_{h})-\widehat{R}(\bar{s}_{h},\pi)-V^{\pi}(\bar{s}_{h+1})\right\rvert+\left\lvert V^{\pi}(\bar{s}_{h+1})-\widehat{V}(\bar{s}_{h+1})\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{6\varepsilon}{H^{2}}+\left\lvert V^{\pi}(\bar{s}_{h+1})-\widehat{V}(\bar{s}_{h+1})\right\rvert\leq\cdots\leq\frac{6\varepsilon}{H}.

This contradicts the statement that
\left\lvert V^{\pi}(s_{h})-\widehat{V}^{\pi}(s_{h})\right\rvert\geq\epsilon_{\mathsf{tol}}-2\varepsilon/H
by the choice of
\epsilon_{\mathsf{tol}}
. So we can conclude that there exists a state
